The infimal convolution structure of the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance
Nicolò De Ponti, Giacomo Enrico Sodini, Luca Tamanini

Abstract
. We show that the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance can be expressed as the metric infimal convolution of the Hellinger and the Wasserstein distances, as conjectured by Liero, Mielke, and Savaré. To prove it, we study with the tools of Unbalanced Optimal Transport the so called Marginal Entropy-Transport problem that arises as a single minimization step in the definition of infimal convolution. Careful estimates and results when the number of minimization steps diverges are also provided, both in the specific case of the Hellinger–Kantorovich setting and in the general one of abstract distances.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 49Q22, 28A33

1.
Introduction

Given a complete and separable metric space
(X,{\sf d})
, the
L^{2}
-Kantorovich–Rubinstein
(also:
Wasserstein
)
distance

\mathsf{W}
between two probability measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
is given by the following minimization problem

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\inf_{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})}\int_{X^{2}}{\sf d}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\,\,\mathrm{,}\;\,

where
\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\subset\mathcal{P}(X^{2})
is the set of
couplings
between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
, i.e. probabilities on the product space with marginals
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
. Problem (
1.1
) belongs to the class of optimal transport problems, with cost function given by
{\sf d}^{2}
: the clever choice of the cost, connected to the underlying distance on the space
X
, ensures that, when restricted to the set of probabilities with finite second moment
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
, the Wasserstein distance
\mathsf{W}_{2}
is complete and separable, length (resp. geodesic) if
{\sf d}
is a length (resp. geodesic) distance. Moreover, the map
x\mapsto\delta_{x}
becomes an isometric embedding of
(X,{\sf d})
into
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X),\mathsf{W}_{2})
. All these remarkable properties make
\mathsf{W}_{2}
the ideal candidate for metrizing the set
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
and studying its analytical and geometrical properties.
We refer to the classic monographs
[
2
,
42
,
43
,
39
,
36
,
37
]
and the more recent
[
1
,
16
]
for a thorough picture of optimal transport and the Wasserstein distance.

The extension of the optimal transport problem to the
unbalanced setting
(i.e., the scenario where
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
may have different total non-negative masses) has been investigated in several works. For instance, the adaptation of the dynamical approach from balanced optimal transport
[
4
]
to the unbalanced case is discussed in
[
31
,
34
,
35
,
10
]
. Static extensions related to the Kantorovich formulation have been studied in
[
8
,
15
]
, while
[
14
]
introduces the concept of optimal partial transport. A broad framework for unbalanced optimal transport problems is presented in
[
41
]
, where methods from
[
40
]
are employed to expand upon ideas initially explored in
[
28
,
11
]
.

We concentrate here on the distance
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
, which was simultaneously introduced in
[
28
,
11
,
25
]
and is referred to as the
Hellinger–Kantorovich
or
Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao
distance. This metric can be defined in multiple ways (see Section
4.2.3
for one such approach) and is widely recognized as the natural extension of the Wasserstein distance to
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, the space of non-negative, finite Radon measures on
X
.

The metric space
(\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}})
is complete and separable, with a topology corresponding to the weak convergence of measures. It retains the geodesic and length-space properties of the underlying space while allowing the map
\mathfrak{C}[X]\ni[x,r]\mapsto r\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
to be an isometric embedding. Here, the
geometric cone

\mathfrak{C}[X]
is equipped with the standard cone distance

{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}([x,r],[y,s])\coloneqq\big({r^{2}+s^{2}-2rs\cos({\sf d}(x,y)\wedge\pi)}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which serves as an appropriate setting for unbalanced optimal transport. This cone structure is simply the quotient space
X\times[0,+\infty)
, where all points of the form
(x,0)
are identified as a single vertex, representing the null measure. Further details can be found in Section
2.3
.

A particularly intuitive way to understand the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance is through its
dynamical formulation
in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

(1.2)

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\inf\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[|{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{t}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}|w_{t}|^{2}\right]{\,\rm d}\mu_{t}{\,\rm d}t\right\},

where the infimum is taken over all triplets
(\mu_{\cdot},{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{\cdot},w_{\cdot})
satisfying the boundary conditions
\mu_{t}|_{t=i}=\mu_{i}
for
i=0,1
and solving the
continuity equation with reaction

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\mathrm{div}({\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{t}\mu_{t})=w_{t}\mu_{t}\,\,\mathrm{.}

This formulation, which generalizes the classical Benamou–Brenier theorem
[
4
]
, highlights how the
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
geometry incorporates both transport of mass via
v
and a growth/shrinking effect represented by
w
.
Indeed, on the one hand, we have that for the Wasserstein distance it holds

(1.3)

\mathsf{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\inf\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{t}|^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{t}{\,\rm d}t\right\},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)\,,

where the infimum is taken among all pairs
(\mu_{\cdot},{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{\cdot})
with
\mu_{t}|_{t=i}=\mu_{i}
for
i=0,1
, and solving

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\mathrm{div}({\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{t}\mu_{t})=0\,\,\mathrm{.}

On the other hand, a similar dynamical formulation holds also for a third classical distance on measures: the Hellinger distance (see also Section
4.2.2
):

(1.4)

\mathsf{He}^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\inf\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tfrac{1}{4}|w_{t}|^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{t}{\,\rm d}t\right\},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where the infimum is taken among all pairs
(\mu_{\cdot},w_{\cdot})
with
\mu_{t}|_{t=i}=\mu_{i}
for
i=0,1
, and solving

(1.5)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=w_{t}\mu_{t}\,\,\mathrm{.}

It has been suggested in
[
28
, Remark 8.19]
that the interplay between formulas (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.4
) can be described by saying that
the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance is the infimal convolution of the Hellinger and the Wasserstein distances
. This idea comes from the fact that, if
M
is a Riemannian manifold and
\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{g}_{2}
are two metric tensors on
M
, we can define the inf-convolution of the Riemannian distances associated to
\mathsf{g}_{1}
and
\mathsf{g}_{2}
as

(1.6)

\displaystyle{\sf d}_{\nabla}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\coloneqq\inf\Bigl\{

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left[|v_{1}(s)|_{\mathsf{g}_{1}(x(s))}^{2}+|v_{2}(s)|_{\mathsf{g}_{2}(x(s))}^{2}\right]{\,\rm d}s\,:

\displaystyle x\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}([0,1];M),\,x(i)=x_{i},\,i=0,1,

\displaystyle\dot{x}(s)=v_{1}(s)+v_{2}(s)\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }s\in(0,1)\Bigr\},\quad x_{0},x_{1}\in M.

In order to give a
purely metric
counterpart of this construction, in the same remark, the authors present a metric notion of infimal convolution between two distances
{\sf d}_{1}
and
{\sf d}_{2}
on a space
X
: for points
z_{0},z_{1}\in X
they set

(1.7)

({\sf d}_{1}\nabla{\sf d}_{2})^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})\coloneqq\liminf_{N\to+\infty}\inf\left\{N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left({\sf d}_{1}^{2}(x^{N}_{i-1},y^{N}_{i})+{\sf d}_{2}^{2}(y_{i}^{N},x_{i}^{N})\right):x_{i}^{N},y_{i}^{N}\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)\right\}\,,

where
\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
is the set of
2N+1
points of the form

(1.8)

\{z_{0}=x_{0}^{N},y_{1}^{N},x_{1}^{N},y^{N}_{2},\dots,y_{N-1}^{N},x_{N-1}^{N},y^{N}_{N},x_{N}^{N}=z_{1}\}\subset X.

They finally conjecture that, applying the above construction to
{\sf d}_{1}\coloneqq\mathsf{He}_{2}
and
{\sf d}_{2}\coloneqq\mathsf{W}_{2}
, the resulting distance
{\sf d}_{1}\nabla{\sf d}_{2}
should be the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
.

The above ansatz, or at least the use of the term
inf-convolution
to describe the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance, has appeared in a variety of papers
[
29
,
21
,
45
,
26
,
19
,
20
,
32
,
23
,
27
,
17
,
18
]
but, up to our knowledge, it has never been proved rigorously. The aim of the present paper is precisely to prove this conjecture, in the following sense:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(X,{\sf d})
be a complete, separable, and geodesic metric space. Then

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=(\mathsf{He}_{2}\nabla\mathsf{W}_{2})(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{ for every }\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X).

A first step to see how in general the Hellinger and Wasserstein distances combine to produce the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance has appeared in
[
21
, Pages 1109-1111]
and we report it here since, in part, it is the starting point of our analysis. Suppose
\mu_{0}=r_{0}\delta_{x_{0}}
and
\mu_{1}=r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}}
with
|r_{0}-r_{1}|\ll 1
and
{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\ll 1
for some
r_{0},r_{1}>0
and
x_{0},x_{1}\in X
. Forcing to interpolate between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
with another Dirac measure of the form
\tilde{\mu}_{0}=r_{1}\delta_{x_{0}}
and using a Taylor expansion, we have that

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle(\mu_{0},\tilde{\mu}_{0})=|\sqrt{r_{1}}-\sqrt{r_{0}}|^{2}\,,\qquad\mathsf{W}_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\mu}_{0},\mu_{1})=r_{1}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\,,

(1.10)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle={\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}([x_{0},\sqrt{r_{0}}],[x_{1},\sqrt{r_{1}}])=|\sqrt{r_{1}}-\sqrt{r_{0}}|^{2}+4\sqrt{r_{0}r_{1}}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)

(1.11)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{He}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\tilde{\mu}_{0})+\mathsf{W}_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\mu}_{0},\mu_{1})+\mathcal{O}\left({\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})|\sqrt{r_{1}}-\sqrt{r_{0}}|\right).

Roughly speaking, this means that the two reaction and transport processes from
\mu_{0}
to
\tilde{\mu}_{0}
and from
\tilde{\mu}_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
can be considered as occurring simultaneously and independently at the infinitesimal level, following the words of
[
21
]
, where it is also stated “justifying or quantifying the above discussion for general measures is an interesting question”.

To answer this question, we start our analysis with the study of the problem

(1.12)

\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)}\left\{\mathsf{He}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)+\mathsf{W}_{2}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1})\right\},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),

which corresponds to one elementary step in the construction of the inf-convolution defined in (
1.7
), and fits in the category of
marginal Entropy-Transport problems
analyzed in
[
28
, Section 3.3. E8]
. We recast the above problem as a general
Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem
(see Section
4.1
) for a suitable cost function
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[X]^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
which can be already inferred by (
1.9
), namely

(1.13)

\mathsf{H}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])=|\sqrt{r_{0}}-\sqrt{r_{1}}|^{2}+r_{1}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1}){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}(r_{0}),\quad[x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[X].

This function encodes the fact that both a change in density and a movement in space are allowed, with the latter being constrained to happen precisely at the same mass-level of the target point. The expression of
\mathsf{H}
in (
1.13
) resembles very much a discretized (and unbalanced) version of the metric speed of a curve in the cone
\mathfrak{C}[X]
: if
\mathfrak{y}
is an absolutely continuous curve with values in the geometric cone, it holds

(1.14)

|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}^{2}=|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,1),

where
|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}^{2}
is the metric speed of the curve
\mathfrak{y}
w.r.t. the cone distance
{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
r_{\eta}
and
x_{\eta}
are the projections of the curve on
[0,+\infty)
and
X
, respectively, and
|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}
is the metric speed of
x_{\mathfrak{y}}
w.r.t.
{\sf d}
. We are going to use this fact together with a useful characterization of the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance (see Theorem
4.4
): if
(X,{\sf d})
is a complete, separable, and geodesic metric space, then, for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, it holds

(1.15)

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\min\int\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\quad\text{ where }\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathfrak{y})={\int_{0}^{1}|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}_{t}|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}t}\,,

where the minimum runs over all the plans
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})))
suitably connecting
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
.

To exploit the form of
\mathsf{H}
in (
1.13
) and the characterization of
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
in (
1.15
), we consider the discretized action of a curve
\mathfrak{y}\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
defined as

\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}(\mathfrak{y})\coloneqq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}((i-1)/N),\mathfrak{y}(i/N))\,,

and we use it in the following strategy to prove separately the two inequalities that give Theorem
1.1
.

(1)

\mathsf{He}_{2}\nabla\mathsf{W}_{2}\leq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
: we consider an optimal plan
\pi
as in (
1.15
) and, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we use it to induce an admissible partition as in (
1.8
) in a way to get that

(\mathsf{He}_{2}\nabla\mathsf{W}_{2})^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq\liminf_{N\to+\infty}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{i-1}^{N},\mu_{i}^{N})\leq\liminf_{N\to+\infty}\int\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}.

We will show that the limit inferior in the right-most term above can be estimated from above with the integral of the action of curves on the cone w.r.t.
\pi
, and thus recover the result. This requires comparing
\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}
with the time-integral of the squared metric derivative as in (
1.14
) and involves, in particular, the careful study of the properties of the support of
\pi
and of its interaction with
\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}
.

(2)

\mathsf{He}_{2}\nabla\mathsf{W}_{2}\geq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
: we consider a minimizing sequence of partitions as in (
1.7
) (denoting
x_{i}^{N}\coloneqq\mu_{i}^{N}
and
y_{i}^{N}\coloneqq\sigma_{i}^{N}
) and, upon replacing each
\sigma_{i}^{N}
with
\nu_{i}^{N}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}\limits\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{i-1}^{N},\mu_{i}^{N})
for
i=1,\dots,N
, we suitably interpolate these points with a plan
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}^{N}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathrm{C}}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})))
which is “piecewise” optimal for the primal formulation of the unbalanced optimal transport problem (see Theorem
4.3
) related to
\mathsf{H}
,
so that

(\mathsf{He}_{2}\nabla\mathsf{W}_{2})^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\lim_{N\to+\infty}\int\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}{\,\rm d}\pi^{N}.

Then we prove that the above limit can be bounded from below by the integral of the action of a curve on the cone w.r.t. some admissible plan as in (
1.15
), thus recovering the desired inequality. This is technically challenging since the lack of compactness of the underlying metric space and the fact that both
\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}^{N}
depend on
N
make it difficult to infer the tightness of the family of plans
({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}^{N})_{N}
and also the behavior of the above limit. These difficulties are overcome by a compact approximation procedure and a fine study of the properties of
\mathcal{A}^{N}_{2}
.

We refer the interested reader to the beginning of Sections
6
and
7
for a more detailed description of the strategy adopted for the proofs of the two inequalities.

The study carried out to prove Theorem
1.1
has revealed the mathematical richness of this problem. Apart from the analysis strictly needed for the proof of the conjecture, in the paper we also investigate the abstract properties of the infimal convolution between general distances. Among other results, we show that this construction leads to a symmetric, non-negative function, though it can be degenerate (i.e., being null between different points) and may fail to satisfy the triangle inequality. A more structured behavior emerges in the case the inf-convolution is performed between (Hilbertian) norms, where the problem aligns naturally with the infimal convolution in convex analysis
[
38
]
. This raises the compelling question of whether there exist other purely metric examples, similar to the Hellinger–Wasserstein case, where this construction yields meaningful distances.

In another direction, we also thoroughly examined the properties of the single-step minimization problem (
1.12
) between the Wasserstein and Hellinger distances. This problem corresponds to an iteration step of the associated Minimizing Movement scheme, making it interesting to explore how our analysis could be connected to the theory of gradient flows
[
2
]
. Notice that a peculiarity of our situation is that the Hellinger distance corresponds to a
non-superlinear
entropy functional, and thus deviates from the conventional framework usually adopted in the huge literature originated by the JKO-approach
[
24
]
.

Finally, throughout the whole manuscript we restricted ourselves to the study of the inf-convolution between the 2-Hellinger and 2-Wasserstein distances, but it is natural to wonder whether a result analogous to Theorem
1.1
holds for any arbitrary exponent
p\in(1,+\infty)
. Namely, if also the
p
-Hellinger–Kantorovich distance, introduced in
[
9
, Section I.2]
and far less studied than the
p=2
counterpart, can be regarded as the (
p
-version of the) inf-convolution of
\mathsf{He}_{p}
and
\mathsf{W}_{p}
. A positive answer in this direction would shed some light on the infinitesimal structure of
p
-Hellinger–Kantorovich distances. However, let us mention that the strategy sketched above for the case
p=2
is not directly applicable when
p\neq 2
: in fact, in the former case we strongly leveraged the fine properties of
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
-geodesics, established in
[
28
,
29
]
and not yet available for
p\neq 2
. Nonetheless, we believe that many of our techniques could be adapted to this latter case.

Plan of the paper

In
Section
2
we report a few preliminaries related to metric spaces and measures therein, with a particular focus on the geometric cone. In
Section
3
we discuss the abstract properties of the infimal convolution construction.
Section
4
is devoted to the presentation of the general framework of Unbalanced Optimal Transport and, in particular, to the definition of the Hellinger, Wasserstein, and Hellinger–Kantorovich distances, and to the proof of a few properties of these distances. In
Section
5
we investigate the marginal Entropy-Transport problem between the Hellinger and the Wasserstein distances.
Section
6
and
Section
7
contain the proofs of the two inequalities mentioned above, leading to the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Finally, in
Appendix
A
we discuss the measurability of the geodesic selection map, and in
Appendix
B
we report some computations related to geodesics on the metric cone.

.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we collect some basic notions related to metric and measure spaces we are going to use in the paper, focusing then on the geometric structure of the cone on a metric space, and curves and measures therein.

2.1.
Metric spaces

An
extended metric space
is a pair
(X,{\sf d})
where
{\sf d}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
is a symmetric function (called
extended distance
) satisfying the triangle inequality and such that
{\sf d}(x,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
. An extended metric space is called a metric space when
{\sf d}
takes only finite values, and in such a case we refer to
{\sf d}
as a
distance
. A curve in a (possibly extended) metric space is a continuous map
\gamma:I\to X
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval, and a curve is called
absolutely continuous
if there exists
m\in L^{1}(I)
such that

(2.1)

{\sf d}(\gamma(t_{0}),\gamma(t_{1}))\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}m(t){\,\rm d}t\quad\textrm{for }t_{0},t_{1}\in I,t_{0}<t_{1}.

We denote by
{\mathrm{C}}(I;(X,{\sf d}))
(resp.
\mathrm{AC}(I;(X,{\sf d}))
) the class of all curves (resp. absolutely continuous curves) from
I
to
(X,{\sf d})
endowed with the topology of uniform convergence.
The metric derivative of an absolutely continuous curve is the Borel function

(2.2)

|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|_{{\sf d}}\coloneqq\limsup_{h\to 0}\frac{{\sf d}(\gamma(t+h),\gamma(t))}{|h|},\quad t\in I.

The class of all absolutely continuous curves
\gamma:I\to X
with
|\gamma^{\prime}(\cdot)|_{{\sf d}}\in L^{2}(I)
will be denoted by
\mathrm{AC}^{2}(I;(X,{\sf d}))
; when
I
is not compact, we also consider the local space
\mathrm{AC}^{2}_{loc}(I;(X,{\sf d}))
, consisting of curves in
{\mathrm{C}}(I;(X,{\sf d}))
whose restriction to every compact subinterval
J\subset I
belongs to
\mathrm{AC}^{2}(J;(X,{\sf d}))
. If
(X,{\sf d})
is a complete and separable metric space, then
\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(X,{\sf d}))
is a Borel set in the space
{\mathrm{C}}([0,1];(X,{\sf d}))
.
A curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to X
taking values in the extended metric space
(X,{\sf d})
is a (minimal, constant-speed)
geodesic
if

{\sf d}(\gamma(t_{0}),\gamma(t_{1}))=|t_{0}-t_{1}|{\sf d}(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))\qquad\forall\,t_{0},t_{1}\in[0,1].

We denote by
\rm Geo((X,{\sf d}))
the space of minimal, constant-speed geodesics in the space
(X,{\sf d})
, which is a closed subset of
{\mathrm{C}}([0,1];(X,{\sf d}))
. The extended metric space
(X,{\sf d})
is a geodesic space if for every
x,y\in X
there exists a geodesic connecting them, while it is called
length space
if the distance
between arbitrary pairs of points can be obtained as the infimum of the length of
the absolutely continuous curves
\gamma:[0,1]\to X
connecting them, where the latter is defined as

\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|_{{\sf d}}\,{\,\rm d}t\,.

The length extended distance induced by the extended distance
{\sf d}
on
X
is the function
{\sf d}_{\ell}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
defined as

(2.3)

{\sf d}_{\ell}(x,y)\coloneqq\inf\{\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)\ :\ \gamma\in\mathrm{AC}([0,1];(X,{\sf d})),\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(1)=y\},

and it can be proved that
(X,{\sf d}_{\ell})
is an extended metric space.

We also introduce the
evaluation map

{\sf e}_{t}:{\mathrm{C}}(I;(X,{\sf d}))\to X
,
t\in I
, which is the map defined as
{\sf e}_{t}(\gamma)\coloneqq\gamma(t).

2.2.
Measures

Let
(X,\tau)
be a Hausdorff topological space. We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
the space of non-negative finite Radon measures on
X
, while
\mathcal{B}(X)
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
X
. By
\mathcal{P}(X)\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
we denote the space of probability measures.
We endow
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
with the narrow topology, i.e. the coarsest topology such that all the maps
\mu\mapsto\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mu
are lower semicontinuous for every
\varphi\in\operatorname{LSC}_{b}(X)
, the space of bounded lower semicontinuous functions.

We recall that whenever
(X,\tau)
is a Polish space, then
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
coincides with the set of all non-negative and finite Borel measures and the narrow topology coincides with the weak topology induced by the duality with continuous and bounded functions. We write
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
to denote the weak convergence of
\mu_{n}
to
\mu.

The
support
of a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
is the set

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\coloneqq\{x\in X\ |\ \forall B\in\tau\ :\ x\in B\Rightarrow\mu(B)>0\},

and we say that
\mu
is
concentrated
on
A\in\mathcal{B}(X)
if
\mu(B)=\mu(A\cap B)
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(X)
.

If
A\subset X
is a Borel set and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, we denote by
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}A\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
the restriction of
\mu
to
A
.

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
and
Y
is another Hausdorff topological space, a map
T:X\to Y
is Lusin
\mu
-measurable if for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
compact set
K_{\varepsilon}\subset X
such that
\mu(X\setminus K_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon
and the restriction of
T
to
K_{\varepsilon}

is continuous. We denote by
T_{\sharp}\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
the
push-forward
measure defined by
T_{\sharp}\mu(B)\coloneqq\mu(T^{-1}(B))
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(Y)
. Recall that Borel measurable maps are Lusin
\mu
-measurable for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, whenever
Y
is Polish.

For every
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
there exist
\sigma\in L^{1}_{+}(X,\mu)
and
\nu^{\perp}
such that

(2.4)

\nu=\sigma\mu+\nu^{\perp},\quad\nu^{\perp}\perp\mu\,.

We call such a decomposition the
Lebesgue decomposition
of the measures
\nu
w.r.t.
\mu
.

A
Dirac measure
centered at a point
x\in X
is the measure
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
defined as

(2.5)

\delta_{x}(A)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1\quad\textrm{if}\ x\in A,\\
0\quad\textrm{if}\ x\notin A,\end{cases}\qquad A\in\mathcal{B}(X).

2.3.
The geometric cone

We define the
geometric cone

\mathfrak{C}[X]
on
X
as the set of pairs
(x,r)
,
x\in X
,
r\geq 0
, identifying all the pairs with
r=0
; more precisely, we set

\mathfrak{C}[X]\coloneqq X\times[0,+\infty)/\mathfrak{R},\quad(x,r)\mathfrak{R}(y,s)\text{ if }\left[(x=y\text{ and }r=s)\text{ or }(r=s=0)\right].

Points in the cone are usually denoted by gothic letters
\mathfrak{y}
or equivalence classes
[x,r]
,
x\in X
,
r\geq 0
. The
vertex
of the cone is the point
\mathfrak{o}
corresponding to the equivalence class
[x,0]
,
x\in X
. The quotient map sending a point
(x,r)\in X\times[0,+\infty)
to its equivalence class
[x,r]
is denoted by
\mathfrak{p}
. On the other hand, the projections on
[0,+\infty)
and
X
are simply defined as

{\sf r}:\mathfrak{C}[X]\to[0,+\infty),\qquad{\sf r}([x,r])\coloneqq r\,,

and

{\sf x}:\mathfrak{C}[X]\to X,\qquad{\sf x}([x,r])\coloneqq\begin{cases}x\ \textrm{ if }r>0\\
\bar{x}\ \textrm{ if }r=0\,,\end{cases}

where
\bar{x}\in X
is some fixed point. We omit the dependence of
{\sf x}
on
\bar{x}
since in the constructions where
{\sf x}
is involved this will be irrelevant. If
0<\varepsilon<R<+\infty
, we also introduce the sets

(2.6a)

\mathfrak{C}_{R}[X]\coloneqq\{[x,r]\in\mathfrak{C}[X]\,:\,0\leq r\leq R\}\,,

(2.6b)

\mathfrak{C}_{R,\varepsilon}[X]\coloneqq\{[x,r]\in\mathfrak{C}[X]\,:\,\varepsilon\leq r\leq R\}\,.

On the cone
\mathfrak{C}[X]
there is a natural notion of topology: for a point
[x,r]
with
x\in X
and
r>0
a fundamental system of neighbourhoods is given by the image through
\mathfrak{p}
of a fundamental system of neighbourhoods at
(x,r)
in
X\times[0,+\infty)
. A fundamental system of neighbourhoods at
\mathfrak{o}
is given by the family of sets
\{\mathfrak{C}_{R}[X],\,R>0\}\,.
Equivalently, the topology of the geometric cone is induced, for every
a\in(0,\pi]
, by the distance
{\sf d}_{a,\mathfrak{C}}:\mathfrak{C}[X]\times\mathfrak{C}[X]\to[0,+\infty)
defined as

(2.7)

\displaystyle{\sf d}_{a,\mathfrak{C}}([x,r],[y,s])

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big({r^{2}+s^{2}-2rs\cos({\sf d}(x,y)\wedge a)}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\,=\left(|r-s|^{2}+4rs\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x,y)\wedge a}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad[x,r],[y,s]\in\mathfrak{C}[X]\,.

The geometric cone
\mathfrak{C}[X]
serves as a model for weighted Dirac measures in
X
, i.e. measures of the form
r\delta_{x}
where
x\in X
and
r\geq 0
, see also (
2.5
); in fact, it can be checked that
\mathfrak{C}[X]
is homeomorphic to this space when endowed with the weak topology (see
[
41
, Lemma 2.4]
for the precise statement).
Separability and completeness of the space
(X,{\sf d})
are inherited by
(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{a,\mathfrak{C}})
. Whenever
(X,{\sf d}\wedge a)
is a length (resp. geodesic) space, also
(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{a,\mathfrak{C}})
is a length (resp. geodesic) space (e.g. see
[
7
, Theorem 3.6.17]
). Moreover, notice that the canonical choice
{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}\coloneqq{\sf d}_{\pi,\mathfrak{C}}
gives that
(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})
is a length (resp. geodesic) space whenever
(X,{\sf d})
is a length (resp. geodesic) space (
[
7
, Theorem 3.6.17]
).

2.4.
Curves on the cone

For a curve
\mathfrak{y}\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
we write

r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)\coloneqq{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}(t)),\quad x_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)\coloneqq{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}(t)),\quad t\in[0,1]\,,

and we denote by
r^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)
the derivative of
r_{\mathfrak{y}}
at time
t
and by
|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}
the
{\sf d}
-metric derivative (cf. (
2.2
)) of
x_{\mathfrak{y}}
at time
t
,
t\in[0,1]
. By
[
28
, Lemma 8.1]
we have that

(2.9)

\mathfrak{y}\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r_{\mathfrak{y}}&\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];\mathbb{R}_{+})\\
x_{\mathfrak{y}}&\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}_{loc}(r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{-1}(0,+\infty);(X,{\sf d})),\end{array}\right.

and moreover it holds

(2.10)

|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}^{2}=|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,1).

Let us assume that
(X,{\sf d})
is a geodesic metric space, so that (
\mathfrak{C}[X]
,
{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})
is too. We now describe the structure of geodesics in the geometric cone:

(1)

In the trivial case
\mathfrak{y}_{0}=\mathfrak{y}_{1}
, then a geodesic connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
is given by
\mathfrak{y}(t)=\mathfrak{y}_{0}
,
t\in[0,1]
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{y}_{0}=\mathfrak{o}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{o}
, then

\mathfrak{y}(t)\coloneqq[{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}),t{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})],\quad t\in[0,1]

is a geodesic connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
; the case
\mathfrak{y}_{1}=\mathfrak{o}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{0}\neq\mathfrak{o}
is analogous.

(3)

If both
\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{o}
and
{\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))\geq\pi
, then

\mathfrak{y}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\left[{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})-\big({\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})+{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\big)t\right]\quad&\text{ if }0\leq t\leq\frac{{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})}{{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})+{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})},\\
\left[{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}),\big({\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})+{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\big)t-{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})\right]\quad&\text{ if }\frac{{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})}{{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})+{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})}\leq t\leq 1,\end{cases}

is a geodesic connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
.

(4)

If both
\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{o}
,
\mathfrak{y}_{0}\neq\mathfrak{y}_{1}
, and
0\leq{\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))<\pi
, then a geodesic connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
is given by

\mathfrak{y}(t)=[x(\theta(t)),r(t)]\quad t\in[0,1],

where
x:[0,{\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))]\to X
is a constant speed geodesic in
X
connecting
{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})
to
{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})
and
\theta:[0,1]\to[0,{\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))]
and
r:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
are given by

\displaystyle r^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(1-t)^{2}{\sf r}^{2}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})+t^{2}{\sf r}^{2}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})+2t(1-t){\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}){\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\cos({\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})))

\displaystyle\quad t\in[0,1],

\displaystyle\cos(\theta(t))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{(1-t){\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{0})+t{\sf r}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\cos({\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})))}{r(t)}

\displaystyle\quad t\in[0,1].

Remark 2.1

(Minimal radius of non-degenerate geodesics)
.

In case
\mathfrak{y}_{0}=[x_{0},r_{0}],\mathfrak{y}_{1}=[x_{1},r_{1}]
are both different from
\mathfrak{o}
and
0\leq d\coloneqq{\sf d}({\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}),{\sf x}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))<\pi
, the radius
r_{\mathfrak{y}}
of the geodesic
\mathfrak{y}
connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
is bounded from below by a strictly positive constant depending on
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
. This is trivial if
\mathfrak{y}_{0}=\mathfrak{y}_{1}
, as by Case (
1
) above
r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)=r_{0}
. If instead
\mathfrak{y}_{0}\neq\mathfrak{y}_{1}
, then it is just a matter of computations (postponed to Appendix
B
in order not to burden the presentation) to show that
r_{\mathfrak{y}}
attains its minimum at

(2.11)

t_{\min}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\coloneqq\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if }\cos(d)\geq\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}},\\
\displaystyle{\frac{r_{0}^{2}-r_{0}r_{1}\cos(d)}{{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})}}\quad&\text{ if }\cos(d)<\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}}\wedge\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}},\\
1\quad&\text{ if }\cos(d)\geq\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}},\end{cases}

with value

(2.12)

r_{\min}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\coloneqq r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t_{\min}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}))=\begin{cases}r_{0}\wedge r_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\cos(d)\geq\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}}\wedge\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}},\\
\displaystyle{\frac{r_{0}r_{1}\sin(d)}{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})}}\quad&\text{ if }\cos(d)<\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}}\wedge\frac{r_{1}}{r_{0}}.\end{cases}

We deduce that

r_{\min}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})>0\quad\text{ if }r_{0}r_{1}>0\text{ and }0\leq d<\pi.

We can also infer (see Appendix
B
) that

(2.13)

r_{\min}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(r_{0}\wedge r_{1})\quad\text{ if }r_{0}r_{1}>0\text{ and }0\leq d\leq\pi/2.

2.5.
Measures and functions on the cone

We define the set of measures on the cone with finite
q
-radial moment,
q\in[1,+\infty)
, as

\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{q}(\mathfrak{C}[X])\coloneqq\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X]):\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X]}{\sf r}^{q}{\,\rm d}\alpha<+\infty\right\},

and the map

\mathfrak{h}^{q}:\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{q}(\mathfrak{C}[X])\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),\quad\mathfrak{h}^{q}(\alpha)\coloneqq{\sf x}_{\sharp}({\sf r}^{q}\alpha).

Note that the map
\mathfrak{h}^{q}
does not depend on the point
\bar{x}
in the definition of
{\sf x}
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, we consider the product cone
\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1}
endowed with the product topology. On the product cone we can consider the projections on the components
\pi^{i}:\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1}\to\mathfrak{C}[X]
sending
([x_{0},r_{0}],\dots,[x_{N},r_{N}])
to
[x_{i},r_{i}]
, the projections on the
N+1
copies of
[0,+\infty)
, and on the
N+1
copies of
X
defined as
{\sf r}_{i}\coloneqq{\sf r}\circ\pi^{i}
and
{\sf x}_{i}\coloneqq{\sf x}\circ\pi^{i}
, for
i=0,\dots,N
. Note that
{\sf x}_{i}
depends on the choice of points
\bar{x}_{i}\in X
, but this will be irrelevant. We introduce the set of measures with finite
q
-the radial moment,
q\in[1,+\infty)
, in the product cone as

\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{q}(\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1})\coloneqq\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1}):\int({\sf r}_{0}^{q}+\dots+{\sf r}_{N}^{q}){\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}<+\infty\right\},

and the maps

\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{q}:\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{q}(\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1})\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(X_{i}),\quad\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{q}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})=({\sf x}_{i})_{\sharp}({\sf r}_{i}^{q}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\quad i=0,\dots,N.

Note that the map
\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{q}
does not depend on the point
\bar{x}_{i}\in X
in the definition of
{\sf x}_{i}
.

Finally, when
N=1
, we set
\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\coloneqq\mathfrak{C}[X]^{2}
and we define, for every
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, the set

(2.14)

\mathfrak{H}^{q}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{q}(\mathfrak{C}[X,X]):\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{q}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})=\mu_{i},\,i=0,1\right\}.

If
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{q}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, we say that
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are the
q
-homogeneous marginals of
\alpha
.

We say that a function
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1}\to[0,+\infty]
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, is

(1)

radially
q
-homogeneous,
q\in[1,+\infty)
, if

\mathsf{H}([x_{0},\lambda r_{0}],\dots,[x_{N},\lambda r_{N}])=\lambda^{q}\mathsf{H}([x_{0},r_{0}],\dots,[x_{N},r_{N}])

for every
[x_{0},r_{0}],\dots,[x_{N},r_{N}]\in\mathfrak{C}[X],\,\lambda>0
;

(2)

radially
q
-convex, if the map

(r_{0},\dots,r_{N})\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{0},r_{0}^{1/q}],\dots,[x_{N},r_{N}^{1/q}])

is convex in
[0,+\infty)^{N+1}
for every
x_{0},\dots,x_{N}\in X
.

In the sequel, we will always assume to work with functions
\mathsf{H}
proper and lower semicontinuous. Under these assumptions, if
\mathsf{H}
is
q
-homogeneous, it satisfies
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o},\dots,\mathfrak{o})=0
.

Finally, let us also provide a simplified definition of dilation tailored to our purposes (see
[
28
]
for the general definition): given
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
q\in[1,+\infty)
, and a constant
\vartheta>0
, the
(\vartheta,q)
-dilation of
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1})
is defined as

(2.15)

\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1})\ni{\rm dil}_{\vartheta,q}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\coloneqq({\rm prd}_{\vartheta})_{\sharp}(\vartheta^{q}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\,,

where

{\rm prd}_{\vartheta}([x_{0},r_{0}],\dots,[x_{N},r_{N}])\coloneqq([x_{0},r_{0}/\vartheta],\dots,[x_{N},r_{N}/\vartheta])\,.

The marginal constraints have a natural scaling invariance w.r.t. dilation maps, in the sense that for every
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1})
it holds

(2.16)

\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{q}({\rm dil}_{\vartheta,q}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}))=\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{q}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\quad\textrm{ for every }i=0,\dots,N\,,

see
[
41
, Lemma 2.8]
. It is also immediate to see that, whenever
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[X]^{N+1}\to[0,+\infty]
is radially
q
-homogeneous,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
q\in[1,+\infty)
, then

(2.17)

\int\mathsf{H}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}=\int\mathsf{H}{\,\rm d}\left({\rm dil}_{\vartheta,q}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\right).

3.
Inf-convolution between general costs

In this section we study the inf-convolution generated by two general cost functions, giving sufficient conditions for the resulting cost to enjoy relevant properties. Since the main aim of this paper is to prove that, for specific choices of the involved costs, one gets the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance as inf-convolution, we focus in particular on the properties satisfied by a distance. We will show how, in general, finiteness, non-degeneracy and the triangle inequality may fail, even if one starts from very regular cost functions.
This also highlights how nicely Hellinger and Wasserstein distances interact to produce the
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
distance, and motivates the investigation of other possible well-behaved examples.

In order to avoid possible misunderstandings, let us state precisely the terminology we shall use throughout the whole section. Given a non-empty set
U
,

•

a
cost
is a non-negative function
{\sf c}:U\times U\to[0,+\infty)
; if
{\sf c}
is allowed to take the value
+\infty
, then we will refer to it as an
extended cost
; an extended cost is
null on the diagonal
if
{\sf c}(z,z)=0
for all
z\in U
; a cost is
symmetric
if
{\sf c}(z_{0},z_{1})={\sf c}(z_{1},z_{0})
for all
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
.

•

an
(extended) semi-distance
satisfies all axioms of an (extended) distance but non-degeneracy; that is, for an extended semi-distance
\varrho
it may happen
\varrho(z_{0},z_{1})=0
for some
z_{0}\neq z_{1}\in U
;

•

an
(extended) semi-norm
satisfies all axioms of an (extended) norm but non-degeneracy; that is, for an extended semi-norm
\|\cdot\|
on a vector space
V
it may happen
\|v\|=0
for some
0\neq v\in V
.

Let
U
be a set and let
{\sf c}_{1}
and
{\sf c}_{2}
be extended costs on
U
; let
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
be fixed and let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. We say that
P=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N};y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in U^{N+1}\times U^{N}
is an
N
-path from
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
if
x_{0}=z_{0}
and
x_{N}=z_{1}
. The collection of
N
-paths from
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
is denoted by
\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
. The energy of an element
P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
between the costs
{\sf c}_{1}
and
{\sf c}_{2}
is defined as

(3.1)

\mathcal{E}_{N}(P)\coloneqq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left({\sf c}_{1}^{2}(x_{i-1},y_{i})+{\sf c}_{2}^{2}(y_{i},x_{i})\right).

Notice that
\mathcal{E}_{N}
depends on
{\sf c}_{1}
and
{\sf c}_{2}
even if this is not explicit in the notation. Sometimes, we represent an
N
-path
P
as in the diagram below, with the arrows giving a natural orientation to the points in the path:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}

Definition 3.1
.

The infimal convolution (inf-convolution, for short) between
{\sf c}_{1}
and
{\sf c}_{2}
is defined as the quantity

({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})\coloneqq\left(\liminf_{N\to+\infty}\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}(P):P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)\right\}\right)^{1/2},\quad z_{0},z_{1}\in U\,.

For reasons that will be apparent in Section
3.1
, we will sometimes refer to the previous definition as the “
metric
infimal convolution”. Let us also stress that, more precisely, (
3.1
) and
{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}
should be referred to as 2-energy and 2-infimal convolution, respectively; the choice of the exponent 2 is motivated by the main example of infimal convolution discussed in this paper: the inf-convolution between the squared 2-Hellinger and 2-Wasserstein distances. However, a priori other choices of exponent are possible, so that more generally (and with obvious adaptations) one could define the
p
-infimal convolution between two extended costs.

First of all, let us discuss some basic properties of the infimal convolution.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
{\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2}
be extended costs on
U
. Then the inf-convolution
{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

Non-negativity:
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})\geq 0
for all
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
.

(ii)

Monotonicity: if
{\sf c}_{1}^{\prime}
and
{\sf c}_{2}^{\prime}
are extended costs on
U
such that
{\sf c}_{i}\leq{\sf c}_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=1,2
, then
{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}\leq{\sf c}_{1}^{\prime}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}^{\prime}
.

If
{\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2}
are null on the diagonal, then it also holds:

(iii)

({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z,z)=0
for any
z\in U
.

(iv)

Symmetry w.r.t. costs:
{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}={\sf c}_{2}\nabla{\sf c}_{1}
.

If
{\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2}
are null on the diagonal and symmetric, then it also holds:

(v)

Symmetry w.r.t. arguments:
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})=({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{1},z_{0})
for all
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
.

Proof.

(i) Non-negativity is clear since for every
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and every
P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
it holds
\mathcal{E}_{N}(P)\geq 0
.

(ii) The property follows since for every
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and every
P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
it holds
\mathcal{E}_{N}(P)\leq\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{N}(P)
, where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
denotes the energy computed between the extended costs
{\sf c}_{1}^{\prime},{\sf c}_{2}^{\prime}
.

(iii) From (i) we know that
{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}(z,z)\geq 0.
By considering for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
the
N
-path
P_{N}=(z,z,\dots,z)\in U^{N+1}\times U^{N}
we have that
\mathcal{E}_{N}(P_{N})=0
and thus
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z,z)=0.

(iv) Given
P_{N}\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
, the idea is to add ‘costless’ initial and final points to
P_{N}
getting the
(N+1)
-path
P_{N+1}
in the diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{N}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{N+1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}

and observe that

\mathcal{E}^{1,2}_{N}(P_{N})=\frac{N}{N+1}\mathcal{E}^{2,1}_{N+1}(P_{N+1})\geq\frac{N}{N+1}\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}^{2,1}_{N+1}(P)\,:\,P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N+1)\right\},

where
\mathcal{E}^{1,2}_{N},\mathcal{E}^{2,1}_{N}
denote the
N
-energies associated with
({\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2})
and
({\sf c}_{2},{\sf c}_{1})
, respectively. This implies
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})\geq({\sf c}_{2}\nabla{\sf c}_{1})(z_{0},z_{1})
and switching the roles of
{\sf c}_{1}
,
{\sf c}_{2}
the opposite inequality follows.

(v) The argument is similar to (iv). Indeed, for a given
N
-path
P_{N}\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
between two fixed points
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
, we define the path
P_{N+1}
as in the previous point (iv) and then its ‘reverse’ path
\tilde{P}_{N+1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{P}_{N+1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{x}_{N+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{y}_{N+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{x}_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{y}_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{y}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{x}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{y}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{x}_{0}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf c}_{1}}

More explicitly, given the
N
-path
P_{N}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{N};y_{1},\dots,y_{N})
with
x_{0}=z_{0}
and
x_{N}=z_{1}
, we define
\tilde{P}_{N+1}\in\mathscr{P}(z_{1},z_{0};N+1)
by setting
\tilde{x}_{0}\coloneqq x_{N}=z_{1}
,
\tilde{x}_{N+1}\coloneqq x_{0}=z_{0}
,
\tilde{x}_{i}\coloneqq y_{N+1-i}
for
i=1,\dots,N
, and
\tilde{y}_{i}\coloneqq x_{N+1-i}
for
i=1,\dots,N+1
. As a consequence,

\mathcal{E}_{N}(P_{N})=\frac{N}{N+1}\mathcal{E}_{N+1}(\tilde{P}_{N+1})\geq\frac{N}{N+1}\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N+1}(P)\,:\,P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{1},z_{0};N+1)\right\}

whence
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})\geq({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{1},z_{0})
. By swapping the roles of
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
, the reverse inequality follows.
∎

In order for the inf-convolution to be a distance, it remains to discuss sufficient conditions to get finiteness, the triangle inequality, and non-degeneracy. Let us start from a sufficient condition to have a finite inf-convolution.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
be two extended distances on
U
. Then

\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}\leq\min\{\varrho_{1,\ell},\varrho_{2,\ell}\}\,,

where
\varrho_{i,\ell}
denotes the length (possibly extended) distance induced by
\varrho_{i}
, see (
2.3
). In particular,
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}
is finite-valued whenever
\varrho_{i}
is a length distance,
i=1
or
i=2
.

Proof.

Using property (iv) of Proposition
3.2
it is enough to show
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}\leq\varrho_{1,\ell}
.
Since
\varrho_{1}\leq\varrho_{1,\ell}
, using property (ii) of Proposition
3.2
we can also assume without loss of generality that
\varrho_{1}
is an extended length distance. So, let
\varrho_{1}
be an extended length distance,
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
and let us prove
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq\varrho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})
. If
\varrho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})=+\infty
there is nothing to prove, so let us suppose
\varrho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})<+\infty
, let
\varepsilon>0
and let
\gamma:[0,1]\to U
be a constant-speed rectifiable curve joining
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
and such that
\ell_{\rho_{1}}(\gamma)<\rho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})+\varepsilon
. Since
\gamma
is of constant-speed, notice that for every
0\leq s<t\leq 1

(3.2)

\frac{\varrho_{1}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))}{t-s}\leq\frac{\ell_{\rho_{1}}(\gamma|_{[s,t]})}{t-s}=\ell_{\rho_{1}}(\gamma)\,.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
be
N+1
points defined as
x_{i}\coloneqq\gamma(\frac{i}{N})
. Notice that
\tilde{P}\coloneqq(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N};x_{1},\dots x_{N})\in U^{N+1}\times U^{N}
is an
N
-path from
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
. We thus have

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}(P):P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)\right\}\leq\mathcal{E}_{N}(\tilde{P})=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varrho_{1}^{2}(x_{i-1},x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\varrho_{1}^{2}(\gamma(\frac{i-1}{N}),\gamma(\frac{i}{N}))}{\frac{i}{N}-\frac{i-1}{N}}\leq\ell_{\rho_{1}}(\gamma)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varrho_{1}(x_{i-1},x_{i})\leq\ell_{\rho_{1}}^{2}(\gamma)<(\varrho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})+\varepsilon)^{2}\,,

where we have used (
3.2
) and the definition of length of a curve. The result follows by sending
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
and by taking the limit inferior with respect to
N
.
∎

However, even starting from finite distances, the resulting inf-convolution may be infinite, as the following simple example shows.

Example 3.4

(Inf-convolution of distances may take the value
+\infty
)
.

Consider a space
U
with at least
2
distinct points and the discrete metrics on
U
, i.e.

\varrho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})=\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{1})=\begin{cases}0\qquad&\textrm{ if }z_{0}=z_{1}\,,\\
1&\textrm{ if }z_{0}\neq z_{1}\,.\end{cases}

Then
(\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})=+\infty
for every
z_{0}\neq z_{1}\in U
. To see this, it is sufficient to notice that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and every
N
-path
P=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N};y_{1},\dots y_{N})\in U^{N+1}\times U^{N}
with
x_{i-1}\neq y_{i}
or
y_{i}\neq x_{i}
for some
i\in 1,\dots,N
, we have
\mathcal{E}_{N}(P)\geq N
. Since every
N
-path
P
connecting two distinct points must be of this form, the proof is concluded.

We give now a sufficient condition for the inf-convolution of general costs to enjoy the triangle inequality.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
{\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2}
be extended costs on
U
. Assume that for every
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
, the infimal convolution
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})(z_{0},z_{1})
is realized as a limit, that is

\text{ for every $z_{0},z_{1}\in U$ the limit }\lim_{N\to+\infty}\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}(P):P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)\right\}\text{ exists}\,.

Then
{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}
satisfies the triangle inequality.

Proof.

We will prove that for every
t\in(0,1)
and for every
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in U
we have

({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})^{2}(z_{0},z_{2})\leq\frac{({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})}{t}+\frac{({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})^{2}(z_{1},z_{2})}{1-t}\,,

then obtaining the triangle inequality by optimizing with respect to
t\in(0,1).

Let
t\in(0,1)
and let us take
\{N_{1,k},P_{1,k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty},\{N_{2,k},P_{2,k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
be such that
P_{i,k}\in\mathscr{P}(z_{i-1},z_{i};N_{i,k})
,
i=1,2
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{N_{i,k}}(P_{i,k})=({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})^{2}(z_{i-1},z_{i})\,,\quad i=1,2\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to+\infty}\frac{N_{2,k}}{N_{1,k}}=\frac{1-t}{t}\,.

Notice that if
P_{1,k}=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N_{1,k}};y_{1},\dots,y_{N_{1,k}})
and
P_{2,k}=(x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{N_{2,k}};y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{N_{2,k}})
then
\tilde{P}_{k}\coloneqq(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N_{1,k}},x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{N_{2,k}};y_{1},\dots,y_{N_{1,k}},y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{N_{2,k}})\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{2};N_{1,k}+N_{2,k})
,
so that

\displaystyle\inf\{\mathcal{E}_{N_{1,k}+N_{2,k}}(P):\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{2};N_{1,k}+N_{2,k})\}\leq\mathcal{E}_{N_{1,k}+N_{2,k}}(\tilde{P}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{1,k}+N_{2,k}}{N_{1,k}}\mathcal{E}_{N_{1,k}}(P_{1,k})+\frac{N_{1,k}+N_{2,k}}{N_{2,k}}\mathcal{E}_{N_{2,k}}(P_{2,k})

and the conclusion follows by taking the limit as
k\to+\infty
on both sides.
∎

We provide here an involved counter-example showing that, in general, even starting from distances, the triangle inequality may fail. In view of Proposition
3.5
, it also provides an example where the inf-convolution is not realized as a limit.

Example 3.6

(Triangle inequality may fail)
.

We construct in steps a space
X
and distances
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
such that the triangle inequality for the inf-convolutions fails.

1st step.
(Definition of the framework) For every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, let
A_{n}\coloneqq\{a_{i,n}\}_{i=0}^{2n}
,
B_{n}\coloneqq\{b_{i,n}\}_{i=0}^{2n}
, and

\tilde{Y}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}A_{n}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}B_{n^{\prime}}\,.

We define
Y\coloneqq\tilde{Y}/\mathfrak{R}
, where the equivalence relation
\mathfrak{R}
is defined as follows: given
x,y\in\tilde{Y}
, we declare
x\,\mathfrak{R}\,y
provided

•

x=a_{0,n}
and
y=a_{0,m}
, for some
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
; the equivalence class of all these points will be denoted by
z_{0}
;

•

x=a_{2n,n}
and
y=b_{0,n^{\prime}}
or vice versa, for some
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
; the equivalence class of all these points will be denoted by
z_{1}
;

•

x=b_{2n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
and
y=b_{2m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
, for some
n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
; the equivalence class of all these points will be denoted by
z_{2}
;

•

x=y
.

When it does not create confusion, we still denote by
a_{i,n}
and
b_{j,n^{\prime}}
the equivalence classes
[a_{i,n}]
and
[b_{j,n^{\prime}}]\in Y
of the elements
a_{i,n}
and
b_{j,n^{\prime}}\in\tilde{Y}
.

We endow
Y
with a graph structure by declaring the neighbors of each point (recall that the neighborhood relationship
\sim
is symmetric):

•

z_{0}\sim a_{1,n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
;

•

z_{1}\sim a_{2n-1,n}
and
z_{1}\sim b_{1,n^{\prime}}
,
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
;

•

z_{2}\sim b_{2n^{\prime}-1,n^{\prime}}
,
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
;

•

a_{i-1,n}\sim a_{i,n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
i=2,\dots,2n-1
;

•

b_{i-1,n^{\prime}}\sim b_{i,n^{\prime}}
,
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
i=2,\dots,2n^{\prime}-1
.

z_{0}
z_{1}
z_{2}
a_{1,1}
b_{1,1}
a_{1,2}
a_{2,2}
a_{3,2}
b_{1,2}
b_{2,2}
b_{3,2}
\vdots
\vdots
a_{n,n}
b_{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
\vdots
\vdots
a_{1,n}
a_{2n-1,n}
b_{1,n^{\prime}}
b_{2n^{\prime}-1,n^{\prime}}

Figure 1.
A representation of the space
Y
.

2nd step.
(Definition of the distances)
We now define two distances
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
on
Y
starting from costs
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}
and
\tilde{\varrho}_{2}
.

We let
\tilde{\varrho}_{u}(y,y)=0
for every
y\in Y,u=1,2
. We then define, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
i=1,\dots,2n
,

\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n}\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ odd}\\
1\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ even}\end{cases}\,,\qquad\qquad\tilde{\varrho}_{2}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})\coloneqq\begin{cases}1\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ odd}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n}\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ even}\end{cases}\,.

In an analogous fashion, for every
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
i=1,\dots,2n^{\prime}
we define

\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(b_{i-1,n^{\prime}},b_{i,n^{\prime}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n^{\prime}}\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ odd}\\
1\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ even}\end{cases}\,,\qquad\qquad\tilde{\varrho}_{2}(b_{i-1,n^{\prime}},b_{i,n^{\prime}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}1\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ odd}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n^{\prime}}\quad&\textrm{if }i\textrm{ even}\end{cases}\,.

The distances
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
between two arbitrary points in
Y
are then defined by length minimization. Namely, for all
x,y\in Y
and
u=1,2
,

(3.3)

\varrho_{u}(x,y)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{\varrho}_{u}(y_{j-1},y_{j})\,:\,m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},\,y_{0}=x,\,y_{m}=y,\,y_{j-1}\sim y_{j}\,\forall j=1,\dots,m\right\}\,.

We call
chain
a collection of points
\{y_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m}
connecting
x
and
y
as in the previous definition, and notice that every
x,y\in Y
are connected by a chain and the infimum above is a minimum.

3rd step.
(Explicit value of
\varrho_{u}
)
Let
x,y\in Y
be such that all the following conditions are violated

C.1

x=y
;

C.2

x\sim y
;

C.3

x=a_{1,n}
and
y=a_{1,m}
for some
n\neq m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
;

C.4

x=a_{2n-1,n}
and
y=a_{2m-1,m}
for some
n\neq m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
;

C.5

x=b_{1,n^{\prime}}
and
y=b_{1,m^{\prime}}
for some
n^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
;

C.6

x=b_{2n^{\prime}-1,n^{\prime}}
and
y=b_{2m^{\prime}-1,m^{\prime}}
for some
n^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.

We call
distant
two points
x,y
as above, and we claim that

(3.4)

\textrm{if }x,y\in Y\ \textrm{are distant, then }\varrho_{u}(x,y)>1,\ u=1,2.

To show this, first of all note that, if
x=a_{2n-1,n}
and
y=b_{1,n^{\prime}}
(or the other way around) for some
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, then
x,y
are distant and, for every chain
\{y_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m}
connecting them, there are indexes
i^{*},j^{*}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
\{y_{i^{*}-1},y_{i^{*}}\}=\{x,z_{1}\}
and
\{y_{j^{*}-1},y_{j^{*}}\}=\{z_{1},y\}
, so that
\varrho_{u}(x,y)>1
.
If instead
x
and
y
do not have this form and are distant, for every chain
\{y_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m}
connecting them, there exists an index
j^{*}\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}
such that
\{y_{j^{*}-1},y_{j^{*}},y_{j^{*}+1}\}=\{a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n},a_{i+1,n}\}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,2n-1\}
, or
\{y_{j^{*}-1},y_{j^{*}},y_{j^{*}+1}\}=\{b_{i-1,n^{\prime}},b_{i,n^{\prime}},b_{i+1,n^{\prime}}\}
for some
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,2n^{\prime}-1\}
. This proves (
3.4
).

Now we show that

(3.5)

{\varrho}_{u}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})=\tilde{\varrho}_{u}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},\ i=1,\dots,2n,\ u=1,2,

and

(3.6)

{\varrho}_{u}(b_{i-1,n^{\prime}},b_{i,n^{\prime}})=\tilde{\varrho}_{u}(b_{i-1,n^{\prime}},b_{i,n^{\prime}})\qquad\forall n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},\ i=1,\dots,2n^{\prime},\ u=1,2.

We actually show the equality
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})=\varrho_{1}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})
, the other cases being analogous. By the definition of
\varrho_{1},
it is clear that
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})\geq\varrho_{1}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})
. For the converse inequality, notice that for every chain
\{y_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m}
connecting
a_{i-1,n}
to
a_{i,n}
we must have an index
j^{*}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
y_{j^{*}-1}=a_{i-1,n}
and
y_{j^{*}}=a_{i,n}
or indexes
j^{*},\overline{j}^{*}\in\{0,\dots,m\}
such that
y_{j^{*}}=z_{0}
and
y_{\overline{j}^{*}}=z_{1}
. In the first case the inequality is immediate, while in the second case we have

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(y_{j-1},y_{j})\geq\varrho_{1}(z_{0},z_{1})>1\geq\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(a_{i-1,n},a_{i,n})
, where we used that
z_{0},z_{1}
are distant.

4th step.
(Bound on
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{1})
and
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
) We introduce the space
X
as the subset of
Y
defined as

X\coloneqq\left(\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}A_{(2k)!}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}B_{(2k^{\prime}+1)!}\right)/\mathfrak{R}\,.

We still denote by
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
the restrictions of
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
to
X
. Notice that what we have proven so far still holds on
X
.

Let us also introduce the sets

X_{A}\coloneqq\left(\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}A_{(2k)!}\right)/\mathfrak{R},\qquad X_{B}\coloneqq\left(\bigsqcup_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}B_{(2k^{\prime}+1)!}\right)/\mathfrak{R}.

We claim now that
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq 1
and
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq 1
. We prove
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq 1
, the other case being analogous. Notice that it is sufficient to prove that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{(2k)!}(P_{(2k)!})=1
, where
\mathcal{E}_{(2k)!}
is the energy between
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
and
P_{(2k)!}\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};(2k)!)
is the
(2k)!
-path from
z_{0}
to
z_{1}
defined as

P_{(2k)!}=(z_{0},a_{2,(2k)!},a_{4,(2k)!},\dots,z_{1};\,a_{1,(2k)!},a_{3,(2k)!},\dots,a_{2(2k)!-1,(2k)!})\,.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{(2k)!}(P_{(2k)!})

\displaystyle=(2k)!\sum_{i=1}^{(2k)!}\left(\varrho_{1}^{2}(a_{2i-2,(2k)!},a_{2i-1,(2k)!})+\varrho_{2}^{2}(a_{2i-1,(2k)!},a_{2i,(2k)!})\right)

\displaystyle=(2k)!\sum_{i=1}^{(2k)!}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(2k)!}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(2k)!}\right)^{2}=1\,,

for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, where we have used (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), and thus the claim is proved.

5th step.
(
\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{2})=+\infty
)
In this final step we will show that

(3.7)

\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{2})=+\infty\qquad\textrm{on }X,

so that

\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{2})=+\infty>2\geq\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{1})+\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\,,

thus providing an example where the inf-convolution fails to satisfy the triangle inequality.

By definition of inf-convolution, we can find
P_{N_{l}}=(x^{l}_{0},x^{l}_{1},\dots,x^{l}_{N_{l}};y^{l}_{1},\dots,y^{l}_{N_{l}})\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{2};N_{l})
such that
N_{l}\to+\infty
as
l\to+\infty
and

\varrho_{1}\nabla\varrho_{2}(z_{0},z_{2})=\lim_{l\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}^{1/2}(P_{N_{l}})\,.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed; it will be useful also to denote

z_{2i}^{l}\coloneqq x_{i}^{l},\quad i=0,\dots,N_{l},\quad z_{2i-1}^{\ell}\coloneqq y_{i}^{\ell},\quad i=1,\dots,N_{l}.

We call
consecutive
two points
v,w\in P_{N_{l}}
such that
v=z_{i}^{l},w=z_{i+1}^{l}
for some
i\in\{0,\dots,2N_{l}-1\}
. If
P_{N_{l}}
contains two consecutive distant points, then from (
3.4
) it immediately follows that
\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(P_{N_{l}})>N_{l}
. If
P_{N_{l}}
contains only non-distant consecutive points, this means that for every pair of consecutive points
(v,w)
in
P_{N_{l}}
it holds that
(v,w)
satisfies one among
\ref{C.1}-\ref{C.6}
. Therefore, only two cases are possible:

(1)

Each pair of consecutive points
(v,w)
in
P_{N_{l}}
satisfies one between
C.1
-
C.2
(and thus does not satisfy any among conditions
C.3
-
C.6
);

(2)

P_{N_{l}}
contains only non-distant consecutive points and there exists at least one pair of consecutive points
(v,w)
in
P_{N_{l}}
that satisfies one among conditions
C.3
-
C.6
.

We show that case (2) can be excluded: let us call
bad
a pair of consecutive points satisfying one among conditions
C.3
-
C.6
; we show that any path
P_{N_{l}}
as in (2) above can be replaced by a path
\tilde{P}_{N_{l}}
with lower energy containing no consecutive bad or distant points, that is
\tilde{P}_{N_{l}}
falls under case (1) above. We show how to do that in a very specific case, that is, when
P_{N_{l}}
as in (2) contains a unique pair of bad points of the form
(v,w)=(z^{l}_{i^{*}},z^{l}_{i^{*}+1})
for some index
i^{*}\in\{0,\dots,2N_{l}-1\}
, satisfying
C.3
(resp.
C.4
); the other cases
C.5
-
C.6
for a single point can be treated in a similar way, and the general case can be achieved with a simple induction argument. Thus, suppose that

•

(v,w)
as above satisfies
C.3
: we set

\tilde{P}_{N_{l}}=(\tilde{z}^{l}_{0},\tilde{z}^{l}_{2},\dots,\tilde{z}^{l}_{2N_{l}};\tilde{z}^{l}_{1},\dots,\tilde{z}^{l}_{2N_{l}-1})\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{2};N_{l})
defined as
\tilde{z}^{l}_{i}=z_{0}
for every index
i\in\{0,\dots,i^{*}\}
, and
\tilde{z}^{l}_{i}=z^{l}_{i}
for every
i\in\{i^{*}+1,\dots,2N_{l}\}
; to see that
\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(\tilde{P}_{N_{l}})\leq\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(P_{N_{l}})
it is enough to observe that
\varrho_{u}(a_{1,n},a_{1,m})\geq\varrho_{u}(z_{0},a_{1,m})
for every
n\neq m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
u=1,2
.

•

(v,w)
as above satisfies
C.4
: let
j^{*}
be the minimal value of
j
such that
z^{l}_{j}\in X_{B}\setminus\{z_{1}\}
. We set
\tilde{P}_{N_{l}}=(\tilde{z}^{l}_{0},\tilde{z}^{l}_{2},\dots,\tilde{z}^{l}_{2N_{l}};\tilde{z}^{l}_{1},\dots,\tilde{z}^{l}_{2N_{l}-1})\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{2};N_{l})
defined as
\tilde{z}_{i}^{l}=z_{i}^{l}
for every
i\in\{0,\dots,i^{*}\}\cup\{j^{*},\dots,2N_{l}\}
, and
\tilde{z}_{i}^{l}=z_{1}
for every
i\in\{i^{*}+1,\dots,j^{*}-1\}
; to see that
\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(\tilde{P}_{N_{l}})\leq\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(P_{N_{l}})
it is enough to observe that
\varrho_{u}(a_{2n-1,n},a_{2m-1,m})\geq\varrho_{u}(a_{2n-1,n},z_{1})
for every
n\neq m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
u=1,2
.

We are left to consider only case (1). It is also easy to see that we can assume that the following does not occur: there are indexes
0\leq i^{*}<j^{*}<l^{*}\leq 2N_{l}
such that
z_{i^{*}}^{l}=z_{l^{*}}^{l}\neq z_{j^{*}}^{l}
. We call
ordered
the paths avoiding this behaviour.
Therefore, we are left to consider the case in which
P_{N_{l}}
is an ordered path for which every pair of consecutive points
(v,w)
is such that
v\sim w
or
v=w
. In particular, there exist indexes
k,k^{\prime},i^{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that

z_{i}^{l}\in A_{(2k)!}\setminus\{z_{1}\}\text{ for every }i\in\{0,\dots,i^{*}-1\}\quad\text{ and }\quad z_{i}^{l}\in B_{(2k^{\prime}+1)!}\text{ for every }i\in\{i^{*},\dots,2N_{l}\}.

Let
j_{l},j^{\prime}_{l}
be the largest numbers in
\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that
2(2j_{l})!\leq i^{*}
and
2(2j^{\prime}_{l}+1)!\leq 2N_{l}-i^{*}
, and set
q_{l}\coloneqq(2j_{l})!
,
q^{\prime}_{l}\coloneqq(2j^{\prime}_{l}+1)!
; note that
k\leq j_{l}
,
k^{\prime}\leq j^{\prime}_{l}
, and
q_{l}+q^{\prime}_{l}\leq N_{l}
. Using the values of
\varrho_{u}
computed in the 3rd step,
u=1,2
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(P_{N_{l}})

\displaystyle=N_{l}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l}}\left(\varrho_{1}^{2}(x^{l}_{i-1},y^{l}_{i})+\varrho_{2}^{2}(y^{l}_{i},x^{l}_{i})\right)\geq(q_{l}+q^{\prime}_{l})\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l}}\left(\varrho_{1}^{2}(x^{l}_{i-1},y^{l}_{i})+\varrho_{2}^{2}(y^{l}_{i},x^{l}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\geq(q_{l}+q^{\prime}_{l})\left[\sum_{i=1}^{(2k)!}2\frac{1}{2((2k)!)^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{(2k^{\prime}+1)!}2\frac{1}{2((2k^{\prime}+1)!)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=(q_{l}+q^{\prime}_{l})\left[\frac{1}{(2k)!}+\frac{1}{(2k^{\prime}+1)!}\right]

\displaystyle\geq(q_{l}+q^{\prime}_{l})\left[\frac{1}{q_{l}}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}_{l}}\right]=2+\frac{(2j_{l})!}{(2j^{\prime}_{l}+1)!}+\frac{(2j^{\prime}_{l}+1)!}{(2j_{l})!}>2+2\max\{j_{l},j^{\prime}_{l}\}

where the last inequality is elementary. Note that to pass from the first to the second line we have used that the part of
P_{N_{l}}
in
X_{A}
must contain at least
2(2k)!
pairs of consecutive points
(v,w)
with
v\neq w
and
\varrho_{u}(v,w)
is bounded from below by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(2k)!}
for every
u=1,2
. The same holds for the part of
P_{N_{l}}
in
X_{B}
, replacing
2k
with
2k^{\prime}+1
.

We conclude that, in general, we have

\mathcal{E}_{N_{l}}(P_{N_{l}})>\min\{N_{l},2+2\max\{j_{l},j^{\prime}_{l}\}\}\to+\infty\quad\text{ as }l\to+\infty.

This shows (
3.7
), and concludes the example.

We introduce now a definition of
stability
for a cost, which will be relevant to understand when a single step of inf-convolution is already sufficient to determine the inf-convolution (see Lemma
3.8
). We also show that the prototypical example of a stable cost is given by a length distance (see Lemma
3.9
).

Definition 3.7
.

An extended cost
{\sf c}:U\times U\to[0,+\infty]
is
stable
provided

(3.8)

{\sf c}^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf\mathcal{F}_{N}(P)\,,

where

\mathcal{F}_{N}(P)\coloneqq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\sf c}^{2}(x_{i-1},x_{i})

and the infimum runs over all
P=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in U^{N+1}
such that
x_{0}=z_{0}
and
x_{N}=z_{1}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
{\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2}
be two extended costs on
U
. If

{\sf c}(z_{0},z_{1})\coloneqq\Big(\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(P)\,:\,P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};1)\right\}\Big)^{1/2},\qquad z_{0},z_{1}\in U

defines a stable extended cost (where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is the energy introduced in (
3.1
) for
N=1
with costs
{\sf c}_{1},{\sf c}_{2}
), then

{\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2}={\sf c}.

Proof.

Let
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
be fixed. The stability of
{\sf c}
and its very definition yield the first and the second identity below, respectively

\displaystyle{\sf c}^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf\left\{N\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\sf c}^{2}(x_{i-1},x_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf\left\{N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y_{i}\in U}\left\{{\sf c}_{1}^{2}(x_{i-1},y_{i})+{\sf c}_{2}^{2}(y_{i},x_{i})\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{i}\in U,\\
i=1,\dots,N\end{subarray}}\left\{N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{{\sf c}_{1}^{2}(x_{i-1},y_{i})+{\sf c}_{2}^{2}(y_{i},x_{i})\right\}\right\}\,,

where, as in Definition
3.7
, the outermost infimum runs over all
(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in U^{N+1}
such that
x_{0}=z_{0}
and
x_{N}=z_{1}
. It is now sufficient to remark that minimizing over all
(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in U^{N+1}
as above and over all
(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in U^{N}
is equivalent to minimizing over all
P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
, so that (taking also (
3.1
) into account) the last line above becomes

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}(P)\,:\,P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)\right\}

and this is exactly
({\sf c}_{1}\nabla{\sf c}_{2})^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})
.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\varrho
be an extended length semi-distance on
U
. Then
\varrho
is a stable extended cost.

Proof.

Let
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
be fixed. If
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
P=(x_{0},\dots,x_{N})\in U^{N+1}
is such that
x_{0}=z_{0}
,
x_{N}=z_{1}
, then

(3.9)

\varrho^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varrho(x_{i-1},x_{i})\right)^{2}\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varrho^{2}(x_{i-1},x_{i})\leq\mathcal{F}_{N}(P)\,.

Taking the infimum over all
(N+1)
-tuples
P
such that
x_{0}=z_{0}
,
x_{N}=z_{1}
and then passing to the
\liminf
as
N\to+\infty
, we get the ‘
\leq
’ inequality in (
3.8
).

For the converse inequality, by definition of length semi-distance we can find, for every
\varepsilon>0
, a Lipschitz curve (which we may assume to be parametrized by arc-length)
\gamma^{\varepsilon}:[0,\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})]\to U
such that
\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})\leq\varrho(z_{0},z_{1})+\varepsilon
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
be arbitrary and let us consider points
x_{i}\coloneqq\gamma^{\varepsilon}(i\ell/N)
for
i=0,\dots,N
, where
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})
for ease of notation. Defining
P\coloneqq(x_{0},\dots,x_{N})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}(P)

\displaystyle=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varrho^{2}(x_{i-1},x_{i})\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{[(i-1)\ell/N,i\ell/N]})^{2}

\displaystyle=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\int_{(i-1)\ell/N}^{i\ell/N}|\dot{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}|(t){\,\rm d}t\right)^{2}=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\ell}{N}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})^{2}\leq(\varrho(z_{0},z_{1})+\varepsilon)^{2}\,.

Considering again the infimum over all
(N+1)
-tuples
P
such that
x_{0}=z_{0}
,
x_{N}=z_{1}
and passing to the liminf as
N\to+\infty
, we deduce that

\liminf_{N\to+\infty}\inf\mathcal{F}_{N}(P)\leq(\varrho(z_{0},z_{1})+\varepsilon)^{2}\,.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, we obtain the ‘
\geq
’ inequality in (
3.8
) and thus the conclusion.
∎

From the combination of the previous two lemmas we can describe the inf-convolution of an extended (length) semi-distance with itself. The following result also gives a sufficient condition for the inf-convolution to be finite and, under stronger assumptions, to enjoy all the properties of a distance.

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
\varrho
be an extended semi-distance on
U
. Then
\frac{\varrho}{\sqrt{2}}\leq\varrho\nabla\varrho
. If in addition
\varrho
is a length semi-distance, then
\varrho\nabla\varrho=\frac{\varrho}{\sqrt{2}}
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows from a variant of
(
3.9
)
. If we fix
z_{0},z_{1}\in U
, take
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
P=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N};y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
, then

(3.10)

\varrho^{2}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varrho^{2}(x_{i-1},x_{i})\leq 2N\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\varrho^{2}(x_{i-1},y_{i})+\varrho^{2}(y_{i},x_{i}))=2\mathcal{E}_{N}(P)\,.

It is now sufficient to take again the infimum over
\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};N)
and then pass to the
\liminf
as
N\to+\infty
to get
\varrho\leq\sqrt{2}\varrho\nabla\varrho
.

Assume now that
\varrho
is a length semi-distance. It suffices to show that

(3.11)

\inf_{z\in U}\left\{\varrho^{2}(z_{0},z)+\varrho^{2}(z,z_{1})\right\}=\frac{1}{2}\varrho^{2}(z_{0},z_{1}).

Indeed, by Lemma
3.9
we know that (the square root of) the right-hand side above is a stable cost. Hence, if this identity holds true, then Lemma
3.8
ensures that the left-hand side is equal to
(\varrho\nabla\varrho)^{2}
, whence the conclusion.

In order to prove (
3.11
), the ‘
\geq
’ inequality is a particular case of (
3.10
) for
N=1
. As regards ‘
\leq
’, we argue similarly to Lemma
3.9
. By definition of length semi-distance we can find, for every
\varepsilon>0
, a Lipschitz curve (which we may assume to be parametrized by arc-length)
\gamma^{\varepsilon}:[0,\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})]\to U
such that
\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})\leq\varrho(z_{1},z_{2})+\varepsilon
. Let us consider the point
z\coloneqq\gamma^{\varepsilon}(\ell/2)
, where
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})
for ease of notation. Setting
P\coloneqq(x_{0},y_{1},x_{1})=(z_{0},z,z_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}(P)

\displaystyle=\varrho^{2}(x_{0},y_{1})+\varrho^{2}(y_{1},x_{1})\leq\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{[0,\ell/2]})^{2}+\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{[\ell/2,\ell]})^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{\ell/2}|\dot{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}|(t){\,\rm d}t\right)^{2}+\left(\int_{\ell/2}^{\ell}|\dot{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}|(t){\,\rm d}t\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ell_{\varrho}(\gamma^{\varepsilon})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\varrho(z_{0},z_{1})+\varepsilon)^{2}.

Taking the infimum over all
P\in\mathscr{P}(z_{0},z_{1};1)
, namely over all
z\in U
, and then letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
gives the conclusion.
∎

3.1.
Metric infimal convolution vs. convex infimal convolution

In this section we compare our notion of (metric) infimal convolution with the
convex
infimal convolution that we are going to recall.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
f,g:V\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be extended real-valued functions. Their convex infimal convolution (convex inf-convolution, for short), denoted by
(f\ \Box\ g):V\to[-\infty,+\infty]
, is defined as

(3.12)

(f\ \Box\ g)(v)\coloneqq\inf\{f(w)+g(z)\ :\ w,z\in V,w+z=v\}=\inf\{f(z)+g(v-z)\ :\ z\in V\}.

We use the terminology
convex
infimal convolution to distinguish it from the
metric
infimal convolution that we have introduced in Definition
3.1
. Notice that the convex inf-convolution corresponds to the classical infimal convolution in the sense of convex analysis (e.g., see
[
38
]
), and in this field it is well known that the convex inf-convolution of two proper, lower semicontinuous and convex functions is again a proper, lower semicontinuous and convex function. The same operation is also called
epi-sum
or
epi-addition
, since the epigraph of the convex inf-convolution of two functions is the Minkowski sum of the epigraphs of those functions.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
be two (extended) semi-norms on
V
. Then
(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}:V\to\mathbb{R}
is an (extended) semi-norm on
V
.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, let us put
\|v\|_{\Box}\coloneqq(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}(v)
.

It is clear that
\|\cdot\|_{\Box}
is non-negative and finite in case the two norms are finite. Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in V
, we have
\|\lambda v\|_{\Box}=|\lambda|\|v\|_{\Box}
since the absolute homogeneity holds for
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
, and

\|\lambda v\|^{2}_{\Box}=\inf\{\|\lambda w\|^{2}_{1}+\|\lambda z\|^{2}_{2}\ :w,z\in V,w+z=v\}.

It remains to prove that
\|\cdot\|_{\Box}
satisfies the triangle inequality, which is equivalent to show that for any
v,w\in V
and for any
t\in(0,1)
it holds

\frac{\|v\|_{\Box}^{2}}{t}+\frac{\|w\|_{\Box}^{2}}{1-t}\geq\|v+w\|_{\Box}^{2}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. By definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\Box}
we can find
v_{1},v_{2},w_{1},w_{2}\in V
, with
v_{1}+v_{2}=v
and
w_{1}+w_{2}=w
, such that

\frac{\|v\|_{\Box}^{2}+\varepsilon}{t}+\frac{\|w\|_{\Box}^{2}+\varepsilon}{1-t}\geq\frac{\|v_{1}\|_{1}^{2}+\|v_{2}\|_{2}^{2}}{t}+\frac{\|w_{1}\|_{1}^{2}+\|w_{2}\|_{2}^{2}}{1-t}\geq\|v_{1}+w_{1}\|_{1}^{2}+\|v_{2}+w_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|v+w\|_{\Box}^{2}\,,

where in the second inequality we have used that
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
satisfy the triangle inequality, and again the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\Box}
in the last inequality since
v_{1}+w_{1}+v_{2}+w_{2}=v+w.
The proof is concluded by sending
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
∎

In the next theorem we obtain that the convex infimal convolution and the metric infimal convolution coincide when computed between norms.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
be two extended semi-norms on
V
. Then for every
v,w\in V
it holds

(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}(v-w)=(\|\cdot\|_{1}\nabla\|\cdot\|_{2})(v,w).

Proof.

By Proposition
3.12
we know that
(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}
is an extended semi-norm, and thus the function

(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}:V\times V\to[0,\infty],\qquad(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}(v,w)\coloneqq(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}(v-w)

is an extended semi-distance on
V
. It is also a length extended semi-distance since the curve

\gamma(t):[0,1]\to V,\qquad\gamma(t)\coloneqq v+t(w-v)

is a length minimizing geodesic connecting two arbitrary vectors
v,w\in V
, as a consequence of the absolute homogeneity of the extended semi-norm
(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}
. In particular, Lemma
3.9
ensures that
(\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}\ \Box\ \|\cdot\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}
is a stable cost. We are thus in position to apply Lemma
3.8
which concludes the proof.
∎

The following example is inspired by
[
44
]
and shows that the conclusion in Proposition
3.12
is sharp, in the sense that we cannot infer that the infimal convolution is a norm even when it is computed between two norms.

Example 3.14

(Inf-convolution may be degenerate)
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
\|\cdot\|_{1}
,
\|\cdot\|_{2}
be two complete but not equivalent norms on
V
(one can take for instance
V=\ell^{2}
,
\|\cdot\|_{1}
be the norm induced by the following scalar product

(v,w)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}v_{n}w_{n}\qquad v=(v_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty},\ w=(w_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\,,

and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
be the standard
\ell^{2}
-norm). Then
\|\cdot\|_{1}\nabla\|\cdot\|_{2}(v,w)=0
for some
v\neq w\in V
