. Indeed, suppose by contradiction that
\|\cdot\|_{1}\nabla\|\cdot\|_{2}
is not degenerate and thus a length distance induced by a norm thanks to Proposition
3.12
and Theorem
3.13
. Let us consider the identity map
\mathsf{id}:(V,\|\cdot\|_{1})\to(V,\|\cdot\|_{2})
. By Proposition
3.3
, the product topology of
(V,\|\cdot\|_{1})\times(V,\|\cdot\|_{2})
is finer than the product topology of
(V,\|\cdot\|_{1}\nabla\|\cdot\|_{2})^{2}
, which is Hausdorff. It follows that
\mathsf{id}
has a closed graph, and thus it is continuous by the closed graph theorem. This gives the desired contradiction, since
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
were supposed to be not equivalent.

In the next proposition we show, under suitable assumption, an explicit representation of the infimal convolution of two
Hilbertian
norms. The formula we obtain is strongly connected to the so-called
parallel addition
of two operators, introduced in the finite-dimensional setting in
[
3
]
and further extended to the case of infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces in
[
33
,
13
]
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
A,B:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be two scalar products with induced norms
\|\cdot\|_{A},\|\cdot\|_{B}
, respectively. Let us suppose that
(V,A)
is a Hilbert space and that there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

(3.13)

\|v\|_{B}\geq C_{1}\|v\|_{A},\qquad|B(v,w)|\leq C_{2}\|v\|_{A}\|w\|_{B}\quad\forall v,w\in V.

Then

(3.14)

(\|\cdot\|_{1}\nabla\|\cdot\|_{2})(v,w)=\|v-w\|_{B}-B(v-w,T^{A,B}(v-w)),

where
T^{A,B}:V\to V
is the unique map such that

(3.15)

A(T^{A,B}v,w)+B(T^{A,B}v,w)=B(v,w)\quad\forall w\in V.

In particular, if
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we are identifying
A
and
B
with two symmetric and positive definite
d\times d
matrices, we have

(\|\cdot\|_{1}\nabla\|\cdot\|_{2})(v,w)=(v-w)^{\intercal}(A^{-1}+B^{-1})^{-1}(v-w).

Proof.

We start by noting that the (bijective and linear) map
T^{A,B}
is well defined as a consequence of the Lax–Milgram lemma, since the form
b(v,w)\coloneqq A(v,w)+B(v,w)
is continuous and strongly coercive and the map
w\mapsto B(v,w)
is linear and continuous with respect to the normed space
(V,\|\cdot\|_{A})
.

The last conclusion follows from (
3.14
) up to noticing the matrices identities

T^{A,B}=(A+B)^{-1}B,\qquad B-B(A+B)^{-1}B=(A^{-1}+B^{-1})^{-1},

where the second one is a consequence of the Woodbury matrix identity.

It remains to prove (
3.14
). By Theorem
3.13
it is sufficient to show

\inf_{z\in V}A(z,z)+B(v-z,v-z)=B(v,v)-B(v,T^{A,B}v)\quad\forall v\in V

or, equivalently, that

(3.16)

\inf_{z\in V}A(z,z)-2B(v,z)+B(z,z)=-B(v,T^{A,B}v)\quad\forall v\in V,

which follows easily since
z=T^{A,B}v
is a minimizer in the left hand side of (
3.16
).
∎

Notice that, under the assumptions of Proposition
3.15
, the resulting infimal convolution is always a norm since, up to a constant, it is bounded from below by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{A}
.

4.
Three distances on measures

In this whole section
(X,{\sf d})
is a metric space. We are going to introduce the Hellinger (or Matusita), the (Kantorovich–Rubinstein–)Wasserstein and the Hellinger–Kantorovich (or Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao) distances on the space of non-negative, finite and Radon measures
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
on
X
. To do so, we adopt the approach of
[
41
]
to Unbalanced Optimal Transport in order to introduce these distances as particular instances of costs on measures.

4.1.
Unbalanced Optimal Transport

Definition 4.1

(Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost)
.

Let
q\in[1,+\infty)
and let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous, and radially
q
-homogeneous function, see Section
2.5
. We define the
q
-
Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost
associated to
\mathsf{H}
as

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H},q}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{q}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\right\},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X).

Remark 4.2

(
2
-homogeneity and
q
-homogeneity)
.

Given
q\in[1,+\infty)
, we define the map
\mathsf{T}_{q}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to\mathfrak{C}[X,X]
as

(4.1)

\mathsf{T}_{q}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq([x_{0},r_{0}^{2/q}],[x_{1},r_{1}^{2/q}]).

It is easy to check that
(\mathsf{T}_{q})_{\sharp}
is a bijective transformation from
\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
to
\mathfrak{H}^{q}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
for any pair
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
. Moreover, if
\mathsf{H}_{q}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty]
is radially
q
-homogeneous, then
\mathsf{H}\coloneqq\mathsf{H}_{q}\circ\mathsf{T}_{q}
is radially
2
-homogeneous and

(4.2)

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{q},q}=\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H},2}.

For this reason, we will simply use the notation
\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}}
in place of
\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H},2}
and limit our exposition to
2
-homogeneous cost functions
\mathsf{H}
.

The following result contains the main properties of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost
[
41
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper, lower semicontinuous, and radially
2
-homogeneous function.

(1)

For every
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
such that
\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})<+\infty
, there exists an optimal
2
-homogeneous coupling

{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}.

Such coupling can be chosen to be a probability concentrated on
\{0\leq{\sf r}_{i}\leq R,\,i=0,1\}
where
R>0
only depends on
\mu_{0}(X)
and
\mu_{1}(X)
.

(2)

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}}
is a lower semicontinuous, subadditive, convex function and satisfies

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}}(r_{0}\delta_{x_{0}},r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}})=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}_{2}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{0},\sqrt{r_{0}}],[x_{1},\sqrt{r_{1}}])

for every
x_{0},x_{1}\in X
and every
r_{0},r_{1}\geq 0
, where
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}_{2}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
is the l.s.c. convex envelope of
(r_{0},r_{1})\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{0},\sqrt{r_{0}}],[x_{1},\sqrt{r_{1}}])
.

4.2.
The three choices of cost function

We now consider three specific choices of cost function
\mathsf{H}
as above inducing the three distances that will play a crucial role in the sequel.

4.2.1.
The
L^{p}
-Wasserstein (extended) distance

Let
p\in[1,+\infty)
and let
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty]
be the proper, lower semicontinuous, and radially
2
-homogeneous function defined as

(4.3)

\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq\begin{cases}r_{0}^{2}{\sf d}^{p}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad&\text{ if }r_{0}=r_{1},\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ else},\end{cases}\quad[x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[X].

The resulting UOT cost induced by
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}}
is the
p
-th power of the
L^{p}
-Wasserstein (extended) distance
\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}

[
2
,
43
,
39
]
which can be also characterized as

\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\left(\inf\left\{\int_{X\times X}{\sf d}^{p}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\right\}\right)^{1/p},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),

where
\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
denotes the set of couplings between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
defined as

\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X\times X):\pi^{i}_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}=\mu_{i},\,i=0,1\},

being
\pi^{i}:X\times X\to X
the projection
\pi^{i}(x_{0},x_{1})\coloneqq x_{i}
for every
(x_{0},x_{1})\in X\times X
,
i=0,1
. Notice that, whenever
\mu_{0}(X)\neq\mu_{1}(X)
, then
\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\emptyset
so that
\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=+\infty
. When
\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})<+\infty
it is well known that the set of optimal couplings
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
of elements
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
realizing the infimum above is not empty (convex and weakly compact), see e.g.
[
40
, Theorem 3.5]
. When we consider the subset of
\mathcal{P}(X)
(the Borel probability measures in
X
) given by

\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X):\int_{X}{\sf d}^{p}(x_{0},x){\,\rm d}\mu(x)<+\infty\text{ for some (hence for every) $x_{0}\in X$}\right\}\,,

then
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X),\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}})
is a metric space. If
(X,{\sf d})
is separable, then
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X),\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}})
is separable. If, additionally,
(X,{\sf d})
is complete (resp. a length space, resp. a geodesic space), then
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X),\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}})
is complete (resp. a length space, resp. a geodesic space). In case
(X,{\sf d})
is complete and separable, the topology of
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X),\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}})
is induced by the following notion of convergence

\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mu\textrm{ in }\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}\Longleftrightarrow\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu\textrm{ and }\int_{X}{\sf d}^{p}(x_{0},x){\,\rm d}\mu_{n}(x)\rightarrow\int_{X}{\sf d}^{p}(x_{0},x){\,\rm d}\mu(x),\ x_{0}\in X.

For the above properties, see e.g.
[
2
, Chapter 7]
.

Finally, notice that
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}}
is radially
2
-convex, so that

\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}}^{p}(r_{0}\delta_{x_{0}},r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}})=\begin{cases}r_{0}{\sf d}^{p}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad&\text{ if }r_{0}=r_{1},\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ else,}\end{cases}\quad x_{0},x_{1}\in X,\,r_{0},r_{1}\geq 0.

4.2.2.
The
p
-Hellinger distance

Let
p\in[1,+\infty)
and let
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}_{p}}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty)
be the proper, lower semicontinuous, and radially
2
-homogeneous function defined as

(4.4)

\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}_{p}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq\begin{cases}(r_{0}^{2/p}-r_{1}^{2/p})^{p}\quad&\text{ if }x_{0}=x_{1},\\
r_{0}^{2}+r_{1}^{2}\quad&\text{ if }x_{0}\neq x_{1},\end{cases}\quad[x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[X].

The resulting UOT cost induced by
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}_{p}}
is the
p
-th power of the
p
-Hellinger distance
\mathsf{He}_{p}
, which can be also characterized as

\mathsf{He}_{p}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\left(\int_{X}\left|\bigg(\frac{{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}}{{\,\rm d}\eta}\bigg)^{1/p}-\bigg(\frac{{\,\rm d}\mu_{1}}{{\,\rm d}\eta}\bigg)^{1/p}\right|^{p}{\,\rm d}\eta\right)^{1/p},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),

where
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
is any measure such that
\mu_{i}\ll\eta
for
i=0,1
. Notice that the above characterization does not depend on
\eta
. The
p
-Hellinger distance is a complete and geodesic distance and induces the topology of the total variation on
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, see e.g.
[
30
]
. By introducing the function

(4.5)

M_{p}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,+\infty]\qquad M_{p}(s)=\begin{cases}(s^{1/p}-1)^{p}&\textrm{if}\ s\geq 0,\\
+\infty&\textrm{otherwise,}\end{cases}

it is easy to show (e.g., see
[
12
, Lemma 3]
) that
\mathsf{He}_{p}^{p}
can be equivalently defined as

(4.6)

\mathsf{He}_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=D_{M_{p}}(\mu_{0}||\mu_{1})\coloneqq\int_{X}M_{p}(\vartheta){\,\rm d}\mu_{1}+\mu_{0}^{\perp}(X)\,,\qquad\mu_{0}=\vartheta\mu_{1}+\mu_{0}^{\perp},

where we have used the Lebesgue decomposition of
\mu_{1}
w.r.t.
\mu_{0}
, see (
2.4
), and thus corresponds to the Csiszár divergence generated by the convex and lower semicontinuous function
M_{p}
,
M_{p}
-divergence for short.

Finally notice that
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}_{p}}
is
2
-radially convex, so that

\mathsf{He}_{p}^{p}(r_{0}\delta_{0},r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}})=\begin{cases}(r_{0}^{1/p}-r_{1}^{1/p})^{p}\quad&\text{ if }x_{0}=x_{1},\\
r_{0}+r_{1}\quad&\text{ if }x_{0}\neq x_{1},\end{cases}\quad x_{0},x_{1}\in X,\,r_{0},r_{1}\geq 0.

4.2.3.
The Hellinger–Kantorovich distance

Let
\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d}}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty)
be the proper, lower semicontinuous, and radially
2
-homogeneous function defined as

(4.7)

\begin{split}\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])&\coloneqq{\sf d}^{2}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\\
&=r_{0}^{2}+r_{1}^{2}-2r_{0}r_{1}\cos({\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi/2),\quad[x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[X],\end{split}

where
{\sf d}^{2}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}
is as in (
2.7
).
The resulting UOT cost induced by
\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d}}
is the square of the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}
. Whenever
(X,{\sf d})
is separable, then
(\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d})
is separable. If, additionally,
(X,{\sf d})
is complete (resp. a length space, resp. a geodesic space), then
(\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d})
is complete (resp. a length space, resp. a geodesic space). In case
(X,{\sf d})
is complete and separable, the topology of
(\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d})
coincides with the weak topology. The above properties can be found in
[
28
, Sections 7,8]
.

Notice that
\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d}}
is radially
2
-convex, so that

(4.8)

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(r_{0}\delta_{0},r_{1}\delta_{1})={\sf d}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}^{2}([x_{0},\sqrt{r_{0}}],[x_{1},\sqrt{r_{1}}])\,.

Moreover, it is not difficult to check that the squared canonical distance on the cone
{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is a proper, lower semicontinuous, and radially 2-homogeneous function whose l.s.c. convex envelope
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}_{2}\left({\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}\right)
coincides with the function

{\sf d}^{2}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}([x_{0},\sqrt{r_{0}}],[x_{1},\sqrt{r_{1}}]),

so that (see e.g.
[
41
, Corollary 3.18]
for a proof) the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance can be recovered minimizing w.r.t. the canonical cone distance i.e.

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}_{{\sf d}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}:{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\right\},\quad\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X).

Let us also recall a useful dynamic formulation of
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
, consequence of
[
28
, Theorem 8.4]
:

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
(X,{\sf d})
be a length complete and separable metric space and let us define the lower semicontinuous functional (the 2-action functional)
\mathcal{A}_{2}:{\mathrm{C}}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\to[0,+\infty]
as

(4.9)

\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathfrak{y})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\int_{0}^{1}|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}_{t}|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}t}\quad&\text{ if }\mathfrak{y}\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})),\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ else,}\end{cases}

where
|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}_{t}|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
is the metric derivative of the curve
\mathfrak{y}
at time
t
, see (
2.2
) and (
2.9
).
Then, for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
it holds

(4.10)

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{\sf d}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\inf\int\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\,,

where the infimum runs over all
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
such that
({\sf e}_{0},{\sf e}_{1})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. If
(X,{\sf d})
is additionally assumed to be geodesic, then the infimum is attained.

We conclude with a slight improvement of
[
28
, Theorem 8.6]
.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
(X,{\sf d})
be a geodesic, complete and separable metric space. For every (constant speed, length-minimizing)
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
there exists a measure
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{P}({\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})))
such that:

(1)

\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{t})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}})=\mu_{t}
for all
t\in[0,1]
;

(2)

it holds

(4.11)

|\mu_{t}^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}}^{2}=\int|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})=\int\left(|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ $t\in[0,1]$}\,;

(3)

(\mathsf{e}_{t})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}
is concentrated on
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[X]\coloneqq\{[x,r]\in\mathfrak{C}[X]\,:\,0\leq r\leq R\}
, for all
t\in[0,1]
, where
R>0
is a constant depending only on
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
;

(4)

\pi
is concentrated on

\left\{\mathfrak{y}\in{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\,:\,r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)>0\,\Rightarrow\,{\sf d}(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(0),x_{\mathfrak{y}}(1))\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

By
[
28
, Theorem 8.6]
, we can find
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{P}({\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C})}))
satisfying points (a)-(c) as above. We show that also point (d) must hold: we argue by contradiction, assuming that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(B)>0
, where

B\coloneqq\left\{\mathfrak{y}\in{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})):r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)>0\text{ and }{\sf d}(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(0),x_{\mathfrak{y}}(1))>\frac{\pi}{2}\right\},

and we produce a curve
(\tilde{\mu}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}))
connecting
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
whose length is strictly less than
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. Let
T_{0},T_{1}:{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\to{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
be defined as

T_{0}(\mathfrak{y})(t)\coloneqq[x_{\mathfrak{y}}(0),(1-t)\sqrt{2}r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)],\quad T_{1}(\mathfrak{y})(t)\coloneqq[x_{\mathfrak{y}}(1),t\sqrt{2}r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)],\quad t\in[0,1],\,\mathfrak{y}\in{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})).

Note that, by construction,
T_{0}(\mathfrak{y})
is the geodesic connecting
\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{y}(0)
to
\mathfrak{o}
and
T_{1}(\mathfrak{y})
is the geodesic connecting
\mathfrak{o}
to
\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{y}(1)
so that

(4.12)

|T_{i}(\mathfrak{y})^{\prime}|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}(t)=2r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i),\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,1),\quad i=0,1.

Observe also that every element
\mathfrak{y}\in B
satisfies

(4.13)

r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(0)+r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(1)<{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathfrak{y}(0),\mathfrak{y}(1))=|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,1).

We define

\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}}\coloneqq{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}B^{c}+\frac{1}{2}(T_{0})_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}B)+\frac{1}{2}(T_{1})_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}B),\quad\tilde{\mu}_{t}\coloneqq\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}}),\quad t\in[0,1].

By construction
\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}}\in\mathcal{P}({\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C})}))
; we show that
\tilde{\mu}_{i}=\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}})=\mu_{i}
for
i=0,1
: indeed, for every
\varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(X)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}})

\displaystyle=\int\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r^{2}_{\mathfrak{y}}(i){\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}}(\mathfrak{y})=\int_{B^{c}}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r^{2}_{\mathfrak{y}}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{B}\varphi(x_{T_{0}(\mathfrak{y})}(i))r^{2}_{T_{0}(\mathfrak{y})}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{B}\varphi(x_{T_{1}(\mathfrak{y})}(i))r^{2}_{T_{1}(\mathfrak{y})}(i)){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})

\displaystyle=\int_{B^{c}}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r^{2}_{\mathfrak{y}}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\int_{B}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})=\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}).

If
t\in(0,1)
belongs to the (full measure) set of times where point (b) is satisfied for
\pi
and both (
4.12
), (
4.13
) hold true, we have

\displaystyle\int|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}}(\mathfrak{y})

\displaystyle=\int_{B^{c}}|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{B}2r^{2}_{\mathfrak{y}}(0){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{B}2r^{2}_{\mathfrak{y}}(1){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}

\displaystyle<\int_{B^{c}}|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\int_{B}|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})

\displaystyle=\int|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})=|\mu_{t}^{\prime}|^{2}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}}={\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\,.

Integrating the above inequality in
[0,1]
we deduce that
(\tilde{\mu}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}))
(see the discussion above
[
28
, Formula (8.20)]
) and that

\ell^{2}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}}((\tilde{\mu}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]})\leq\int_{0}^{1}|\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{t}|^{2}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}}{\,\rm d}t\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int|\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}}(\mathfrak{y})<{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\,,

namely the sought contradiction.
∎

4.3.
Contraction and invariance properties of UOT

The following is a simple contraction property of these three distances w.r.t. Lipschitz transformations, see also
[
28
, Lemma 8.22]
.

Lemma 4.6

(Contraction property of UOT)
.

Let
(X,{\sf d}_{X})
and
(Y,{\sf d}_{Y})
be metric spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be a
1
-Lipschitz function, i.e.

{\sf d}_{Y}(f(x_{0}),f(x_{1}))\leq{\sf d}_{X}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad\text{ for every }x_{0},x_{1}\in X.

Then for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, we have

•

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}_{Y}}(f_{\sharp}\mu_{0},f_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\leq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}_{X}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
,

•

\mathsf{He}_{p,Y}(f_{\sharp}\mu_{0},f_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\leq\mathsf{He}_{p,X}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
,

•

\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}_{Y}}(f_{\sharp}\mu_{0},f_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\leq\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}_{X}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
,

where we added a subscript
X
(resp.
Y
) to denote the
p
-Hellinger distance in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
).

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{H}_{Z}
be any among
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}_{Z}}}
,
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}_{p,Z}}
or
\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}_{Z}}}
for
Z
equal to
X
or
Y
(again we added a subscript in the notation for
\mathsf{He}_{p}
). Let
T_{f}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to\mathfrak{C}[Y,Y]
be defined as

T_{f}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq([f(x_{0}),r_{0}],[f(x_{1}),r_{1}]).

It is easy to check that
\mathsf{H}_{Y}\circ T_{f}\leq\mathsf{H}_{X}
and, if
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
for measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, then
(T_{f})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(f_{\sharp}\mu_{0},f_{\sharp}\mu_{1})
. We deduce that for any
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, we have

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{Y}}(f_{\sharp}\mu_{0},f_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[Y,Y]}\mathsf{H}_{Y}{\,\rm d}[(T_{f})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}]=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}(\mathsf{H}_{Y}\circ T_{f}){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{X}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}.

Passing to the infimum in
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, we conclude.
∎

As a consequence we get that any of the above three distances is invariant for isometric embeddings, when the metric spaces are complete.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
(X,{\sf d}_{X})
and
(Y,{\sf d}_{Y})
be metric spaces,
(X,{\sf d}_{X})
complete, and let
\iota:X\to Y
be an isometry onto its image, in the sense that

{\sf d}_{Y}(\iota(x_{0}),\iota(x_{1}))={\sf d}_{X}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad\text{ for every }x_{0},x_{1}\in X.

Then for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
, we have

•

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}_{Y}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})={\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}_{X}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
,

•

\mathsf{He}_{p,Y}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})=\mathsf{He}_{p,X}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
,

•

\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}_{Y}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})=\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}_{X}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
,

where we added a subscript
X
(resp.
Y
) to denote the
p
-Hellinger distance in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
).

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.6
, we denote by
\mathsf{H}_{Z}
any among
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}_{p,{\sf d}_{Z}}}
,
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}_{p,Z}}
or
\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}_{Z}}}
for
Z
equal to
X
,
Y
or
\iota(X)\subset Y
(again we added a subscript in the notation for
\mathsf{He}_{p}
).
Since
\iota
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have by Lemma
4.6
that

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{Y}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\leq\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{X}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{ for every }\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X).

We are left to prove the reverse inequality. We observe that

(4.14)

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\subset\mathfrak{C}[\iota(X),\iota(X)]\qquad\textrm{ for every }{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[Y,Y]).

Indeed,
\iota
is an isometry and
(X,{\sf d}_{X})
is complete, so that it is a closed map, hence
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{i})\subset\iota(\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu_{i}))\subset\iota(X)
,
i=0,1
. Therefore, (
4.14
) follows if we prove
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})\subset V_{0}\times V_{1}
, where we have introduced

V_{i}\coloneqq\{[y,r],\,y\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{i}),\,r>0\}\cup\{\mathfrak{o}_{Y}\},\quad i=0,1.

Let
(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})
and let us prove w.l.o.g. that
\mathfrak{y}_{0}\in V_{0}
: if
\mathfrak{y}_{0}=\mathfrak{o}_{Y}
, then
\mathfrak{y}_{0}\in V_{0}
by definition. If instead
\mathfrak{y}_{0}\neq\mathfrak{o}_{Y}
, then
\mathfrak{y}_{0}=[y_{0},r_{0}]
for some
y_{0}\in Y
and some
r_{0}>0
. Assume by contradiction, that
y_{0}\notin\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0})
. Hence we can find a Borel function
\varphi:Y\to[0,1]
and a radius
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on the open ball
{\mathrm{B}}_{{\sf d}_{Y}}(y_{0},\varepsilon)
and

\int_{Y}\varphi{\,\rm d}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0})=0.

However, setting
A\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}({\mathrm{B}}_{{\sf d}_{Y}}(y_{0},\varepsilon)\times(r_{0}/2,3/2r_{0}))\times\mathfrak{C}[Y]
, we have

0=\int_{Y}\varphi{\,\rm d}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[Y,Y]}(\varphi\circ{\sf x}_{0}){\sf r}_{0}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\geq\int_{A}(\varphi\circ{\sf x}_{0}){\sf r}_{0}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\geq\frac{1}{4}r_{0}^{2}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}(A)>0,

where we have used that
A
is an open neighbourhood of
(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}})
. The proof of (
4.14
) is thus concluded and we can immediately derive that

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{Y}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})=\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\iota(X)}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\quad\text{ for every }\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\

by definition of Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost.
We now consider the map
\iota^{-1}
which is
1
-Lipschitz as a map from
(\iota(X),{\sf d}_{Y})
to
(X,{\sf d}_{X})
and thus, using again Lemma
4.6
, for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
we have

\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{X}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{X}}(\iota^{-1}_{\sharp}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0}),\iota^{-1}_{\sharp}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1}))\leq\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\iota(X)}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})=\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{Y}}(\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\iota_{\sharp}\mu_{1})

which gives the desired reverse inequality and concludes the proof.
∎

5.
Marginal entropy-transport problems

In this section we assume a metric space
(X,{\sf d})
to be fixed and we use the notation
\mathsf{He}\coloneqq\mathsf{He}_{2}
,
\mathsf{W}\coloneqq\mathsf{W}_{2,{\sf d}}
,
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}\coloneqq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}
. We devote this section to the study of the energy
\mathcal{E}_{1}
in (
3.1
) when
N=1
,
{\sf c}_{1}=\mathsf{He}
and
{\sf c}_{2}=\mathsf{W}
. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 5.1

(
\mathsf{He}-\mathsf{W}
minimizing movement)
.

We define the marginal
\mathsf{He}-\mathsf{W}
problem between
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
as

(5.1)

\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\coloneqq\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)}\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1}).

The terminology is inspired by
[
28
, Section 3.3 E.8]
, since (
5.1
) enters in the framework of the marginal Entropy-Transport problems in the sense of Liero, Mielke, and Savaré, with transport cost given by
{\sf d}^{2}
and relative entropy generated by the function
M_{2}
as in (
4.5
).

We collect in the following result a few immediate properties of
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

The function
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
has the following properties:

(1)

\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})<+\infty
for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
;

(2)

if
\mu_{i}
is the null measure, then
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\mu_{1-i}(X)
realized by
\nu=\mu_{i}
, for
i=0,1
;

(3)

introducing
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty)
defined as

(5.2)

\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq\begin{cases}(r_{0}-r_{1})^{2}+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad&\text{ if }r_{0}>0,\\
r_{1}^{2}\quad&\text{ if }r_{0}=0,\end{cases}

then
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is a well defined, proper, lower semicontinuous and radially
2
-homogeneous function such that
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}=\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}}
. In particular, for every pair
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
there exists
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that

\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}};

(4)

for every pair
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
the set of
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
realizing the infimum in the definition of
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
is not empty.

Proof.

Claim (1) follows by considering the competitor
\nu\coloneqq\mu_{1}
and the finiteness of
\mathsf{He}
.

Claim (2) is obvious from the definitions of
\mathsf{He}
and
\mathsf{W}
.

To prove claim (3) we start by noticing that for
r_{1}=0
we have
(r_{0}-r_{1})^{2}+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})=r_{0}^{2}
. Since for
r_{0}=0

\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is also independent on the spatial variables, this implies that
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is well defined on
\mathfrak{C}[X,X].
It is clear that
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is proper, while the radially
2
-homogeneity can be easily checked since for every
\lambda>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},\lambda r_{0}],[x_{1},\lambda r_{1}])

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\lambda r_{0}-\lambda r_{1})^{2}+\lambda^{2}r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad&\text{ if }r_{0}>0\\
\lambda^{2}r_{1}^{2}\quad&\text{ if }r_{0}=0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lambda^{2}(r_{0}-r_{1})^{2}+\lambda^{2}r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\hskip 17.07164pt&\text{ if }r_{0}>0\\
\lambda^{2}r_{1}^{2}\hskip 17.07164pt&\text{ if }r_{0}=0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]).

To prove the lower semicontinuity of
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
, we note that in general

(5.3)

\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\geq|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}\quad\text{ for every }[x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[X]

with equality if
r_{0}r_{1}=0
. We consider any sequence
(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{n},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{n})_{n}=([x_{0}^{n},r_{0}^{n}],[x_{1}^{n},r_{1}^{n}])_{n}\subset\mathfrak{C}[X,X]
converging to
(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1})=([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\in\mathfrak{C}[X,X]
as
n\to+\infty
. Using the description of the topology on
\mathfrak{C}[X,X]
we can infer that
r_{i}^{n}\to r_{i}
as
n\to+\infty
,
i=0,1
. Thus, if
r_{0}r_{1}=0
, we can use (
5.3
) and see that

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{n},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{n})\geq\liminf_{n\to+\infty}|r_{0}^{n}-r_{1}^{n}|^{2}=|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}=\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}).

If, on the other hand,
r_{0}r_{1}>0
, for
n
large enough we have
r_{0}^{n}r_{1}^{n}>0
so that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{n},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{n})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to+\infty}|r_{0}^{n}-r_{1}^{n}|^{2}+(r_{1}^{n})^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0}^{n},x_{1}^{n})

\displaystyle=|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})=\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}(\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}).

We now show that
\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}}=\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
. We prove separately the two inequalities, starting with
\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}}\leq\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
. Let
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
; if
\mathsf{W}(\nu,\mu_{1})=+\infty
there is nothing to prove, so let us assume
\mathsf{W}(\nu,\mu_{1})<+\infty
and let

\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}^{ac}+\mu_{0}^{\perp}=\vartheta\nu+\mu_{0}^{\perp}

be the Lebesgue decomposition of
\mu_{0}
w.r.t.
\nu
. Let
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\nu,\mu_{1})
(cf. Section
4.2.1
) and let us define

{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\coloneqq([\pi^{0},\vartheta\circ\pi^{0}],[\pi^{1},1])_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}+([\text{id}_{X},1],\mathfrak{o})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}^{\perp},

where
\pi^{i}
are the projections from
X\times X
to
X
and
\text{id}_{X}
is the identity map on
X
.
It is not difficult to check that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, so that

\displaystyle\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{X\times X}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},\vartheta(x_{0})],[x_{1},1]){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}(x_{0},x_{1})+\int_{X}1{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\vartheta>0\}}\left(\left|\vartheta\circ\pi^{0}-1\right|^{2}+{\sf d}^{2}\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}+\int_{\{\vartheta=0\}}1{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}+\mu_{0}^{\perp}(X)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(\vartheta-1\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\nu+\mu_{0}^{\perp}(X)+\int_{\{\vartheta>0\}}{\sf d}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1}),

where we have also used (
4.6
). This gives the desired inequality since
\nu
is arbitrary. To prove the converse inequality, let now
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
be optimal for the definition of
\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
(cf. Theorem
4.3
(1)) and let us define

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq({\sf x}_{1})_{\sharp}({\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\{{\sf r}_{0}=0\}})\,,\quad{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\coloneqq({\sf x}_{0},{\sf x}_{1})_{\sharp}({\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}})+(\operatorname{id}_{X},\operatorname{id}_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{1}^{\prime}\,,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\nu\coloneqq\pi^{0}_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\,,\quad\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}}\coloneqq([{\sf x}_{0},{\sf r}_{0}],[{\sf x}_{0},{\sf r}_{1}])_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}})+(\mathfrak{o},[\operatorname{id}_{X},1])_{\sharp}\mu_{1}^{\prime}\,.

It is not difficult to check that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma(\nu,\mu_{1})
and
\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)
. Moreover we have

(5.5)

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}|{{\sf r}_{0}}-{{\sf r}_{1}}|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\,,

where
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}}
is as in (
4.4
); indeed:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{0},r_{1}]){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])+\int_{X}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}}(\mathfrak{o},[x,1]){\,\rm d}\mu_{1}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}}|{\sf r}_{0}-{\sf r}_{1}|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}+\int_{X}1{\,\rm d}\left(({\sf x}_{1})_{\sharp}({\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\{{\sf r}_{0}=0\}})\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}}|{\sf r}_{0}-{\sf r}_{1}|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}+\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}=0\}}{\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}|{\sf r}_{0}-{\sf r}_{1}|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{UOT}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}}\left(|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+{\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}({\sf x}_{0},{\sf x}_{1})\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}+\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}=0\}}{\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}+\int_{\{{\sf r}_{0}>0\}}{\sf r}_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}({\sf x}_{0},{\sf x}_{1}){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{He}}}{\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}}+\int_{X\times X}{\sf d}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1}).

Moreover, the above computations show that the measures
\nu
defined in (
5.4
) satisfies claim (4).
∎

Remark 5.3
.

We notice that the function
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
defined in (
5.2
) is not the square of a distance on
\mathfrak{C}[X]
. Indeed, it is not symmetric since
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\neq\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{0},r_{0}])
whenever
r_{0}=1,r_{1}=2
and
{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})=1
. Also the triangle inequality fails: for this, one can consider a triple of points
[x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]
satisfying the admissible constraints

r_{0}=r_{2}>0,\quad r_{1}=0,\quad{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{2})\leq{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})+{\sf d}(x_{1},x_{2}),\quad{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{2})>4.

It is immediate to see that for these points

\sqrt{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{2},r_{2}])}>\sqrt{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])}+\sqrt{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])}.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}}
be as in (
4.7
) and let
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
be as in (
5.2
). Then

\mathsf{H}_{{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}}\leq{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{2}\leq 2\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}\,.

Proof.

The first inequality is obvious, we only consider the second one.
If
r_{0}=0
both functions are equal to
r_{1}
so that we have only to consider the case
r_{0}>0
:

\displaystyle{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cos_to_sin}}}{{=}}|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+4r_{0}r_{1}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\,\,=|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+4(r_{0}-r_{1}+r_{1})r_{1}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\,\,=|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+2\left(r_{0}-r_{1}\right)\left[2r_{1}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\,\,\quad+4r_{1}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\,\,\leq|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+4r_{1}^{2}\sin^{4}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})

\displaystyle\,\,\leq 2|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+4r_{1}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\wedge\pi}{2}\right)+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})

\displaystyle\,\,\leq 2|r_{0}-r_{1}|^{2}+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})+r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})

\displaystyle\,\,=2\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}]).

∎

Although the following results are not needed in the proof of the equality
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}=\mathsf{He}\nabla\mathsf{W}
, we believe they are of interest for future investigations on Marginal Entropy-Transport problems and help to better clarify the properties of the
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
cost. The first result describes minimizers in the definition of
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
in (
5.1
).

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
and let
\nu
be a minimizer for
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
, i.e. such that

\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1})=\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

Let
\nu=\nu^{ac}+\nu^{\perp}
be the Lebesgue decomposition of
\nu
w.r.t.
\mu_{0}
. Then for every
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\nu,\mu_{1})
(that is,
\gamma
is optimal for
\mathsf{W}(\nu,\mu_{1})
) we have that its disintegration
\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}\}_{x\in X}
w.r.t.
\nu
satisfies

{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}=\delta_{x}\quad\text{ for $\nu^{\perp}$-a.e.\penalty\ $x\in X$}.

Remark 5.6
.

This is to say that the singular part of
\nu
w.r.t.
\mu_{0}
does not need to be moved when matching
\nu
to
\mu_{1}
in an optimal way.

Proof.

Let
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\nu,\mu_{1})
; we assume by contradiction that there exists a Borel set
A\subset X
such that
\nu^{\perp}(A)>0
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}\neq\delta_{x}
for every
x\in A
. Let us denote by
\vartheta
the density of
\nu^{ac}
w.r.t.
\mu_{0}
and let us define

\tilde{\mu_{1}}\coloneqq\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\left(\int_{X}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}{\,\rm d}\nu^{\perp}(x)\right),\quad\tilde{\nu}\coloneqq\nu^{ac}+\tilde{\mu_{1}},\quad\tilde{\mu_{1}}=:\vartheta^{\prime}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp},\quad\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}\coloneqq\int_{X}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}{\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)+\int_{X}\delta_{y}{\,\rm d}\tilde{\mu_{1}}(y).

We have for every
\varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(X)
that

\displaystyle\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}(\pi^{0}_{\sharp}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}})=\int_{X\times X}(\varphi\circ\pi^{0}){\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(x){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)+\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(x){\,\rm d}\delta_{y}(x){\,\rm d}\tilde{\mu_{1}}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}+\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\tilde{\mu_{1}}=\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\tilde{\nu},

\displaystyle\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}})=\int_{X\times X}(\varphi\circ\pi^{1}){\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)+\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(y){\,\rm d}\delta_{y}(x){\,\rm d}\tilde{\mu_{1}}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)+\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\tilde{\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)+\int_{X}\int_{X}\varphi(y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{\perp}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X\times X}\varphi(y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}(x,y)=\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mu_{1}.

We deduce that
\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}\in\Gamma(\tilde{\nu},\mu_{1})
. Notice that

\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y)=\int_{\{y\neq x\}}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y)>0\quad\text{ for every }x\in A,

since
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(\{y\neq x\})={\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(\{y:{\sf d}^{2}(x,y)>0\})>0
being
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}\neq\delta_{x}.
We deduce that

\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{\perp}(x)\geq\int_{A}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{\perp}(x)>0.

Therefore, using (
4.6
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\tilde{\nu})+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\tilde{\nu},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta+\vartheta^{\prime}}\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp}(X)+\int_{X\times X}{\sf d}^{2}{\,\rm d}\tilde{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(1+\vartheta+\vartheta^{\prime}-2\sqrt{\vartheta+\vartheta^{\prime}}\right){\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp}(X)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)+\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}\delta_{y}(x){\,\rm d}\tilde{\mu_{1}}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}\left(1+\vartheta+\vartheta^{\prime}-2\sqrt{\vartheta}\right){\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp}(X)+\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(1+\vartheta+\vartheta^{\prime}-2\sqrt{\vartheta}\right){\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp}(X)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu(x)-\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{\perp}(x)

\displaystyle<\int_{X}\left(1+\vartheta+\vartheta^{\prime}-2\sqrt{\vartheta}\right){\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp}(X)+\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta}\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\int_{X}\vartheta^{\prime}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}^{\perp}(X)+\int_{X}\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,y){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta}\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\tilde{\mu_{1}}(X)+\mathsf{W}(\nu,\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta}\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\mathsf{W}(\nu,\mu_{1})+\int_{X}\int_{X}1{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}(y){\,\rm d}\nu^{\perp}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta}\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\nu^{\perp}(X)+\mathsf{W}(\nu,\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1}).

This contradicts the minimality of
\nu
.
∎

Thanks to Proposition
5.5
we can explicitly compute the optimal
\nu
in case of Dirac measures.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
x_{0},x_{1}\in X
,
r_{0},r_{1}\in[0,+\infty)
and let
\mu_{0}\coloneqq r_{0}\delta_{x_{0}}
and
\mu_{1}\coloneqq r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}}
. Then the unique optimal
\nu
for
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is given by

\nu=s_{0}\delta_{x_{0}}+(r_{1}-s_{0})\delta_{x_{1}},\quad s_{0}\coloneqq\begin{cases}r_{1}\wedge\frac{r_{0}}{{\sf d}^{4}(x_{0},x_{1})}\quad&\text{ if }x_{0}\neq x_{1},\\
r_{1}\quad&\text{ if }x_{0}=x_{1}.\end{cases}

In particular

\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(r_{0}\delta_{x_{0}},r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}})=\begin{cases}(\sqrt{r_{0}}-\sqrt{r_{1}})^{2}+r_{1}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}{\sf d}^{4}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq r_{0},\\
r_{0}+r_{1}-\frac{r_{0}}{{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})}\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}{\sf d}^{4}(x_{0},x_{1})>r_{0}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We know that in case
r_{0}=0
or
r_{1}=0
the minimizer is
\nu\coloneqq\mu_{1}
; let us assume that both
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
are strictly larger than
0
. Let
\nu
be a minimizer for
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, let
(\nu^{ac},\nu^{\perp})
be its Lebesgue decomposition w.r.t.
\mu_{0}
and notice that
\nu^{ac}=r\delta_{x_{0}}
for some
r\in[0,r_{1}]
, since
\nu^{ac}\ll\mu_{0}
and
\nu(X)=\mu_{1}(X)=r_{1}
. The unique optimal transport plan
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma(\nu,\mu_{1})
is simply
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}=\nu\otimes\delta_{x_{1}}
so that its disintegration
\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}\}_{x\in X}
w.r.t.
\nu
is simply given by
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}=\delta_{x_{1}}
for every
x\in X
. By Proposition
5.5
, we know that
\nu^{\perp}
is concentrated on the set

\{x\in X:{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}_{x}=\delta_{x}\}=\{x\in X:\delta_{x_{1}}=\delta_{x}\}=\{x_{1}\}.

We deduce that
\nu^{\perp}=(r_{1}-r)\delta_{x_{1}}
. In case
x_{0}=x_{1}
we thus get
s_{0}=r_{1}
; let us assume that
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
: we can explicitly compute
\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)
and
\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{{\,\rm d}\nu^{ac}}{{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}}}\right)^{2}{\,\rm d}\mu_{0}+\nu^{\perp}(X)=r_{0}(1-\sqrt{r/r_{0}})^{2}+(r_{1}-r)=r_{0}+r_{1}-2\sqrt{r_{0}r},

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{X\times X}{\sf d}^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}=\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,x_{1}){\,\rm d}\nu(x)=r{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1}).

We are then left with the minimization problem

\text{ minimize }\quad r{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})-2\sqrt{r_{0}r}\quad\text{ subject to }\quad 0\leq r\leq r_{1},

which gives the desired result.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

By Theorem
4.3
(2), we have that the lower semicontinuous and convex envelope of the
1
-homogeneous version of
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is thus given by

(5.6)

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}_{2}\left(\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}\right)([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])=\begin{cases}(\sqrt{r_{0}}-\sqrt{r_{1}})^{2}+r_{1}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}{\sf d}^{4}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq r_{0},\\
r_{0}+r_{1}-\frac{r_{0}}{{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})}\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}{\sf d}^{4}(x_{0},x_{1})>r_{0}.\end{cases}

The same result could be obtained directly: let
x_{0},x_{1}\in X
and set
d\coloneqq{\sf d}(x_{0},x_{1})\geq 0
; by Fenchel–Moreau duality Theorem, we need to compute the (restriction to
[0,+\infty)^{2}
of the) biconjugate of the function

f_{d}(r_{0},r_{1})\coloneqq\begin{cases}(\sqrt{r_{0}}-\sqrt{r_{1}})^{2}+r_{1}d^{2}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}(r_{0})\quad&\text{ if }r_{0},r_{1}\geq 0,\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Since
f_{d}
is
1
-homogeneous, it is not difficult to see that

f^{*}_{d}(s_{0},s_{1})\coloneqq\sup\left\{r_{0}s_{0}+r_{1}s_{1}-(\sqrt{r_{0}}-\sqrt{r_{1}})^{2}+r_{1}d^{2}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}(r_{0}):r_{0},r_{1}\geq 0\right\}=I_{C_{d}},

that is,
f^{*}_{d}
is the indicator of the set

C_{d}\coloneqq\left\{(s_{0},s_{1}):s_{0}<1,s_{1}\leq 1,s_{1}-1-d^{2}\leq\frac{1}{s_{0}-1}\right\}.

Therefore the biconjugate function
f^{**}_{d}
is obtained by computing

f^{**}_{d}(r_{0},r_{1})\coloneqq\sup\left\{r_{0}s_{0}+r_{1}s_{1}:(s_{0},s_{1})\in C_{d}\right\}=r_{0}+r_{1}-\inf\{r_{0}x+r_{1}y:x>0,y\geq 0,y+d^{2}\geq 1/x\}.

The infimum above is clearly equal to
0
in case
r_{1}r_{0}=0
, so that we can restrict to the case
r_{0}r_{1}>0
;
in this case, the constrained minimization problem in two variables is solved looking at the behaviour of the objective function on the boundary of the domain of minimization, that is, one has to minimize the function
b:(0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

b(x)=\begin{cases}r_{0}x+\frac{r_{1}}{x}-r_{1}d^{2}\quad&\text{ if }0<x\leq\frac{1}{d^{2}},\\
r_{0}x\quad&\text{ if }x\geq\frac{1}{d^{2}},\end{cases}

where we mean that, in case
d=0
, only the first case occurs. In case
d=0
, we have that the minimum is attained at
x=\sqrt{r_{1}/r_{0}}
equal to
2\sqrt{r_{0}r_{1}}
. In case
d>0
, we have two cases:

(1)

If
r_{1}d^{4}\leq r_{0}
, then
b
is decreasing in
(0,\sqrt{r_{1}/r_{0}})
and increasing in
(\sqrt{r_{1}/r_{0}},+\infty)
. Thus the minimum is attained at
x=\sqrt{r_{1}/r_{0}}
equal to
2\sqrt{r_{0}r_{1}}-r_{1}d^{2}
.

(2)

If
r_{1}d^{4}>r_{0}
, then
b
is decreasing in
(0,1/d^{2})
and increasing in
(1/d^{2},+\infty)
. Thus the minimum is attained at
x=1/d^{2}
equal to
r_{0}/d^{2}
.

Putting together this information, one obtains precisely the expression in (
5.6
) for
f^{**}_{d}
.

6.
\mathsf{He}\nabla\mathsf{W}\leq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}

In this section we assume
(X,{\sf d})
to be a geodesic, complete and separable metric space;
we adopt the same notation
\mathsf{He},\mathsf{W},{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}},\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
of the previous section, removing the dependence on the exponent
p=2
and on the distance
{\sf d}
when this does not cause confusion. In this section we also use the notation
\mathcal{E}_{N}
as in (
3.1
) meaning that
{\sf c}_{1}=\mathsf{He}
and
{\sf c}_{2}=\mathsf{W}
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
be fixed; we aim to show that

(6.1)

(\mathsf{He}\nabla\mathsf{W})^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

By Theorem
4.5
, given a (constant speed, length minimizing)
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
, we can find a plan
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{P}({\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C})}))
such that

(6.2)

{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}},

where
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is as in (
4.9
); therefore, the natural strategy is to consider, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}_{>1}
, a family of
N-1
equidistributed points along
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
and, then, to construct an
N
-path by interpolating those points with
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}
minimizers: we aim then to show that the corresponding energy
\mathcal{E}_{N}
can be controlled in the limit by
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. More in detail, we set

(6.3)

\sigma_{i}^{N}\coloneqq\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i/N})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),\qquad i=0,\dots,N

and

{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}_{i}^{N}\coloneqq(\mathsf{e}_{\frac{i-1}{N}},\mathsf{e}_{\frac{i}{N}})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X,X]),\qquad i=1,\dots,N

and, for every
i=1,\dots,N
, we select
\nu_{i}^{N}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
minimizer for
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\sigma_{i-1}^{N},\sigma_{i}^{N})
, whose existence is given by Proposition
5.2
(4). We thus define
P_{N}\coloneqq(\sigma_{0}^{N},\sigma_{1}^{N},\dots\sigma_{N}^{N};\nu_{1}^{N},\dots,\nu_{N}^{N})
and note that
P_{N}\in\mathscr{P}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1};N)
.

Observe that it holds

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{N}(P_{N})&=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{He}^{2}(\sigma^{N}_{i-1},\nu^{N}_{i})+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu^{N}_{i},\sigma^{N}_{i})\right)\\
&=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\sigma^{N}_{i-1},\sigma^{N}_{i})\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}^{N}_{i},\end{split}

where
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is as in (
5.2
). By definition of infimal convolution, therefore, the final inequality (
6.1
) is achieved if we prove that

(6.4)

(\mathsf{He}\nabla\mathsf{W})^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq\limsup_{N\to+\infty}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}^{N}_{i}\leq\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}},

where we also used (
6.2
). Rewriting the term in the middle in terms of
\pi
we have

\displaystyle N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}^{N}_{i}

\displaystyle=\int N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}\left(\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{0}^{1}\left[|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\left(\frac{\lceil tN\rceil}{N}\right)\right|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\right]{\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})

\displaystyle=:\iint_{0}^{1}D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}=:I_{N},

where we have employed the explicit expression of
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
(cf. (
5.2
)), and we have also used Jensen’s inequality (more details will be given in the proof of Theorem
6.3
). What makes
D_{2}^{N}
different from the metric derivative on the cone (cf. (
2.10
)) is the fact that the term multiplying
|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}
should be replaced by
r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)^{2}
, but one can infer that, as
N\to+\infty
and by continuity of
\mathfrak{y}
, the latter will indeed appear, so that

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}I_{N}=\limsup_{N\to+\infty}\iint_{0}^{1}D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\leq\iint_{0}^{1}\left[|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(t)|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\right]{\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}=\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}},

which leads precisely to (
6.4
). To make this strategy rigorous, however, one would need a (uniformly in
N
) integrable control on the ratio

(6.5)

\frac{r_{\mathfrak{y}}\left(\frac{\lceil tN\rceil}{N}\right)}{r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)},

in order to apply e.g. the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem (notice that a constant bound on
r_{\mathfrak{y}}\left(\frac{\lceil tN\rceil}{N}\right)
would not suffice since we do not know whether
(\mathfrak{y},t)\mapsto|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}
belongs to
L^{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}[0,1])
). To control the ratio in (
6.5
), we consider the set of ‘non-degenerate’ geodesics

(6.6)

A\coloneqq\left\{\mathfrak{y}\in{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\,:\,0<{\sf d}(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(0),x_{\mathfrak{y}}(1))\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\,\textrm{ and }\,r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)>0\right\}

and, for any
\varepsilon>0
, that of ‘
\varepsilon
-good’ geodesics

(6.7)

A_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{\mathfrak{y}\in A\,:\,\frac{|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|}{|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(s)|}\leq 2\textrm{ for all }t,s\in[0,1]\textrm{ such that }|t-s|<\varepsilon\right\}.

Therefore, on the set
A_{\varepsilon}
the dominated convergence theorem can be applied, obtaining

(6.8)

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{1}D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\leq\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}.

We will observe that on the set
{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\setminus A
both the metric derivative and
D_{2}^{N}
are equal to
|r^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}
so that no limit in
N
is actually necessary, hence

(6.9)

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}\int_{{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\setminus A}\int_{0}^{1}D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})=\int_{{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\setminus A}\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}.

It remains to control the integral of
D_{2}^{N}
on the set
A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}
: unfortunately, no uniformly integrable control of
D_{2}^{N}
is available on this set, so that we need to modify the plan
\pi
into an ‘approximated’ plan
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
defined as

(6.10)

{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A_{\varepsilon}\cup({\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\setminus A))+T_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon})),

where

T:{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\to\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
,
\mathfrak{y}\mapsto T(\mathfrak{y})
, is defined as

T(\mathfrak{y})(t)\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}[x_{\mathfrak{y}}(0),(1-2t)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)]&\textrm{if }t\in[0,1/2],\\[5.0pt]
[x_{\mathfrak{y}}(1),(2t-1)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)]&\textrm{if }t\in(1/2,1].\end{array}\right.

We can thus repeat the above construction from (
6.3
) to (
6.9
) word by word replacing
\pi
with
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
(and thus
\sigma_{i}^{N}
with
\sigma_{i}^{N,\varepsilon}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}_{i}^{N}
with
{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}_{i}^{N,\varepsilon}
,
\nu_{i}^{N}
with
\nu_{i}^{N,\varepsilon}
,
P_{N}
with
P_{N,\varepsilon}
, and
I_{N}
with
I_{N}^{\varepsilon}
): the argument to reach the inequalities (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) on the sets
A_{\varepsilon}
and
{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\setminus A)
, respectively, is indeed the same. On the other hand, on the set
T(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon})
, we can show that
D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y})
is bounded by
|r^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}+o(N)
, and that the metric derivative coincides with
|r^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}
, therefore obtaining also

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}\int_{T(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon})}\int_{0}^{1}D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})\leq\int_{T(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon})}\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}.

Putting together this latter inequality with (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) written for
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
one finally arrives to

(\mathsf{He}\nabla\mathsf{W})^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq\limsup_{N\to+\infty}I_{N}^{\varepsilon}=\limsup_{N\to+\infty}\iint_{0}^{1}D_{2}^{N}(t,\mathfrak{y}){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}\leq\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}\pi_{\varepsilon}.

It is then sufficient to show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}=\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}
, see Lemma
6.2
.

We now detail the above strategy: as said at the beginning, we fix measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
and we select a (constant speed, length-minimizing)
{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
. By Theorem
4.5
there exists
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\in\mathcal{P}({\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})))
satisfying conditions (a)-(d) in the statement of the same theorem.

Let us point out that the definitions of
A
and
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) are meaningful since for a geodesic
\mathfrak{y}\in A
the radius
r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)
is positive at any
t\in[0,1]
, as explained in Remark
2.1
. As a consequence of the same observation we have that
A_{\varepsilon}\subset A_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\subset A
whenever
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
and
\cup_{\varepsilon>0}A_{\varepsilon}=A
. Finally, we briefly remark that both
A
and
A_{\varepsilon}
are Borel sets: indeed,
A
can be expressed as intersection of the pre-images of the intervals
(0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and
(0,+\infty)
via the Borel maps
{\sf d}({\sf x}\circ{\sf e}_{0},{\sf x}\circ{\sf e}_{1})
and
{\sf r}\circ{\sf e}_{0}\cdot{\sf r}\circ{\sf e}_{1}
respectively; on the other hand, since
t\mapsto r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)
is continuous on
[0,1]
,

A_{\varepsilon}=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}t,s\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]\\
|t-s|<\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\left\{\mathfrak{y}\in A\,:\,\frac{|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|}{|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(s)|}\leq 2\right\}

and each set on the right-hand side is Borel being the pre-image of
[0,2]
via a well-defined (by Remark
2.1
) Borel map, thus settling the measurability of
A_{\varepsilon}
.

As a first step, let us prove that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
(when projected) still joins
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
be defined as in (
6.10
); then

{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})))
and
\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon})=\mu_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

Proof.

The fact that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
is concentrated on
\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
follows by the fact that
\pi
is concentrated on
{\rm Geo}((\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
and (
2.9
). Moreover, since the total mass is preserved by push-forward, we also see that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}})))=1
.

As for
\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon})=\mu_{i}
, fix
\varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(X)
and note that, for
i=0,1
,

\begin{split}\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon})&=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X]}(\varphi\circ{\sf x}){\sf r}^{2}{\,\rm d}(e_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}=\int\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&=\int_{A_{\varepsilon}\cup A^{c}}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\int\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}T_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}))(\mathfrak{y})\\
&=\int_{A_{\varepsilon}\cup A^{c}}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\int_{A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(i))r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&=\int_{A_{\varepsilon}\cup A^{c}}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})+\int_{A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&=\int\varphi(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i))r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{2}(i){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X]}(\varphi\circ{\sf x}){\sf r}^{2}{\,\rm d}(\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\\
&=\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}})=\int_{X}\varphi{\,\rm d}\mu_{i},\end{split}

where on the fourth line we used the fact that
x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(i)=x_{\mathfrak{y}}(i)
and
r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(i)=r_{\mathfrak{y}}(i)
by construction and on the last line the fact that
\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}})=\mu_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{2}:{\mathrm{C}}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))\to[0,+\infty]
be defined as in (
4.9
) and let
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
be as in (
6.10
). Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}=\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}.

Proof.

Since
\pi
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
are concentrated on
\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
only takes finite values on the support of these probabilities.
We then rewrite

\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}=\int_{A_{\varepsilon}\cup A^{c}}\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}+\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}T_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}))

and observe that the first term on the right-hand side converges to
\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
by monotone convergence: indeed,
\mathcal{A}_{2}\geq 0
and
A_{\varepsilon}\uparrow A
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

Thus, we are only left to prove that the second term vanishes as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. To this end, noticing that
T(\mathfrak{y})\in\mathrm{AC}^{2}([0,1];(\mathfrak{C}[X],{\sf d}_{\mathfrak{C}}))
with
|r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}^{\prime}(t)|=2|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)|
on
[0,1/2]
,
|r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}^{\prime}(t)|=2|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)|
on
(1/2,1]
, and
|x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}=0
a.e. in
[0,1]
, yields

\begin{split}\int\mathcal{A}_{2}{\,\rm d}T_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}))&=\int_{A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(|r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+|r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(t)|^{2}|x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{\sf d}\Big){\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&=2\int_{A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}}\big(|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)|^{2}+|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)|^{2}\big){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\leq 4R^{2}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}),\end{split}

where in the last inequality we used the fact that, by Theorem
4.5
(c),
(\mathsf{e}_{0})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}},(\mathsf{e}_{1})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}
are concentrated on
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[X]
. The fact that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
then follows again by
A_{\varepsilon}\uparrow A
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
∎

We are now in the position to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
(X,{\sf d})
be a complete, separable and geodesic metric space and let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
. Then

(\mathsf{He}_{2}\nabla\mathsf{W}_{2,{\sf d}})(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq{\mathsf{H}\!\!\mathsf{K}}_{{\sf d}}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and consider
{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}
as defined in (
6.10
). For every
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
with
N>1/\varepsilon
we also define

\sigma_{i}^{N,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathfrak{h}^{2}((\mathsf{e}_{i/N})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X),\qquad i=0,\dots,N

and

{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}_{i}^{N,\varepsilon}\coloneqq(\mathsf{e}_{\frac{i-1}{N}},\mathsf{e}_{\frac{i}{N}})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[X,X]),\qquad i=1,\dots,N

and, for every
i=1,\dots,N
, we select
\nu_{i}^{N,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
minimizer for
\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\sigma_{i-1}^{N,\varepsilon},\sigma_{i}^{N,\varepsilon})
, whose existence is given by Proposition
5.2
(4). We thus define
P_{N,\varepsilon}\coloneqq(\sigma_{0}^{N,\varepsilon},\sigma_{1}^{N,\varepsilon},\dots\sigma_{N}^{N,\varepsilon};\nu_{1}^{N,\varepsilon},\dots,\nu_{N}^{N,\varepsilon})
and note that
P_{N,\varepsilon}\in\mathscr{P}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1};N)
.

After this premise, we start observing that by construction

(6.11)

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{N}(P_{N,\varepsilon})&=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{He}^{2}(\sigma^{N,\varepsilon}_{i-1},\nu^{N,\varepsilon}_{i})+\mathsf{W}^{2}(\nu^{N,\varepsilon}_{i},\sigma^{N,\varepsilon}_{i})\right)\\
&=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}(\sigma^{N,\varepsilon}_{i-1},\sigma^{N,\varepsilon}_{i})\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}^{N,\varepsilon}_{i},\end{split}

where
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}
is as in (
5.2
). If we introduce for ease of notation the functions
\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}:\mathfrak{C}[X,X]\to[0,+\infty)
defined as

\mathsf{H}^{\prime}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq(r_{0}-r_{1})^{2},\qquad\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}([x_{0},r_{0}],[x_{1},r_{1}])\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r_{1}^{2}{\sf d}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})&\textrm{if }r_{0}>0\\
0&\textrm{if }r_{0}=0\end{array},\right.

so that
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}=\mathsf{H}^{\prime}+\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}
, then we can rewrite the last term above as

(6.12)

\begin{split}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[X,X]}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{W}\mathsf{He}}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}^{N,\varepsilon}_{i}&=\int N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\left(\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&\quad+\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&\quad+\int_{A^{c}}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&\quad+\int N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right){\,\rm d}T_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}))(\mathfrak{y}).\end{split}

Let us now discuss separately the four integrals, that for sake of brevity we shall denote
I_{1}^{N}
,
I_{2}^{N}
,
I_{3}^{N}
, and
I_{4}^{N}
respectively. As regards the first one, by (
2.9
) first and Jensen’s inequality then we obtain

\begin{split}I_{1}^{N}&=\int N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big)-r_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i-1}{N}\Big)\right|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})\leq\int N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\int_{\frac{i-1}{N}}^{\frac{i}{N}}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|{\,\rm d}t\right|^{2}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&\leq\int\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\frac{i-1}{N}}^{\frac{i}{N}}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}{\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})=\int\int_{0}^{1}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}{\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y})\end{split}

and observe that the right-hand side does not depend on
N
any longer, so that

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}I_{1}^{N}\leq\int\int_{0}^{1}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}{\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{y}).

As for
I_{2}^{N}
, note that for any geodesic
\mathfrak{y}\in A
, and a fortiori in
A_{\varepsilon}
, both
\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{o}
, so that by Remark
2.1
it holds
\min_{t\in[0,1]}r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)>0
. This fact, together with (
2.9
) and Jensen’s inequality again, implies that

\begin{split}I_{2}^{N}&=\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big)\right|^{2}{\sf d}^{2}\left(x_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big),x_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i-1}{N}\Big)\right){\,\rm d}\pi(\mathfrak{y})\\
&\leq\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big)\right|^{2}\left|\int_{\frac{i-1}{N}}^{\frac{i}{N}}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}{\,\rm d}t\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&\leq\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big)\right|^{2}\int_{\frac{i-1}{N}}^{\frac{i}{N}}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{\sf d}{\,\rm d}t{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
&=\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\left(\frac{\lceil tN\rceil}{N}\right)\right|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}{\,\rm d}t{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y}).\end{split}

Dividing and multiplying the integrand function by
|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}
(which is possible since, as already pointed out, for any geodesic
\mathfrak{y}\in A_{\varepsilon}
we have
\min_{t\in[0,1]}r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)>0
), we can exploit the definition of
A_{\varepsilon}
, because
|\frac{\lceil tN\rceil}{N}-\frac{1}{N}|<\frac{1}{N}<\varepsilon
for every
t\in[0,1]
, and this yields

\left|r_{\mathfrak{y}}\left(\frac{\lceil tN\rceil}{N}\right)\right|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\leq 4\chi_{A_{\varepsilon}}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2},\qquad\forall t\in[0,1],\,\forall\mathfrak{y}\in A_{\varepsilon}.

Since
\chi_{A_{\varepsilon}}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\leq|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|_{\sf d}^{2}\in L^{1}({\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}[0,1])
as a consequence of (
4.11
), by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem we conclude that

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}I_{2}^{N}\leq\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{1}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{\sf d}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y}).

For the third integral in (
6.12
), by Theorem
4.5
(d), if
\mathfrak{y}\in A^{c}
, then only two situations may occur:

•

either
r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)=0
, so that
x_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)
is constant in time (since
\mathfrak{y}
falls within case (
1
) or case (
2
) discussed in Section
2.4
);

•

or
r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)>0
and
{\sf d}(x_{\mathfrak{y}}(0),x_{\mathfrak{y}}(1))=0
, so that
x_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)
is constant in time.

In both cases

r^{2}_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{{\sf d}}=\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),\mathfrak{y}\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right)=0\qquad\text{for every }i=1,\dots,N,\text{ and a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,1),

so that

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}I_{3}^{N}=0=\int_{A^{c}}\int_{0}^{1}|r_{\mathfrak{y}}(t)|^{2}|x_{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}_{\sf d}{\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y}).

Finally, the fourth integral in (
6.12
) is given by

I_{4}^{N}=\int_{A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\left(T(\mathfrak{y})\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right),T(\mathfrak{y})\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})

and we will now discuss separately the case when
N
is even and when
N
is odd. In the latter, we first remark that for all
\mathfrak{y}\in A
(which ensures
r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(0)=r_{\mathfrak{y}}(0)>0
and
r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(1)=r_{\mathfrak{y}}(1)>0
) it holds
r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(i/N)>0
for all
i=0,\dots,N
by definition of
T
. Thus, by definition of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}

\begin{split}I_{4}^{N}&=\int_{A\setminus A_{\varepsilon}}N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|r_{T(\mathfrak{y})}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big)\right|^{2}{\sf d}^{2}\left(x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}\Big(\frac{i}{N}\Big),x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}\Big(\frac{i-1}{N}\Big)\right){\,\rm d}{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}}(\mathfrak{y})\\
\end{split}

and we observe that all but one terms
{\sf d}(x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}(i/N),x_{T(\mathfrak{y})}((i-1)/N))
equal 0; more precisely, since
N
